VII
VISION STATEMENT
The vision of the Central Bank of Kenya is to be 
a World Class Modern Central Bank.
THE BANK’S MISSION
To formulate and implement monetary policy 
for price stability, foster a stable market-based 
financial system and ensure a sound national 
payment system.
THE BANK’S MANDATES
Article 231 of the Constitution of Kenya and 
Sections 4 and 4A of the Central Bank of Kenya 
(CBK) Amendment Act, 2026 outlines the key 
mandate of CBK as to: -
i. Formulate and implement Monetary Policy 
directed to achieving and maintaining 
stability in the general level of prices.
ii. Foster the stability, resilience, liquidity, 
solvency, integrity and proper functioning 
of a market-based financial system.
iii. Foster the soundness, safety, and effective 
regulation of the banking system.
iv. Formulate and implement such policies 
as best to promote the establishment, 
regulation, and supervision of efficient 
and effective payment, clearing and 
settlement systems.
v. Design, issue and manage eff icient 
distribution of quality currency (notes and 
coins) that are easily accepted and secure 
against counterfeiting.
vi. Support the Government’s economic and 
financial policies including its objectives 
for growth and employment
The other mandates of CBK are: -
vii. Formulating and implementing foreign 
exchange policy. 
viii. Effective management of the nation’s 
foreign exchange reserves. 
ix. Licensing and supervising authorized 
dealers, digital credit providers and 
mortgage refinance companies. 
x. Act as banker and advisor to, and fiscal 
agent of the Government.
M I S S I O N O F B A N K S U P E R V I S I O N 
DEPARTMENT
To promote and maintain the safety, soundness, 
and integrity of the banking system through 
implementation of policies and standards that 
are in line with international best practices for 
bank supervision and regulation.
THE BANK’S CORE VALUES
i) Professionalism:   T h e B o a r d , 
Management and staff will diligently 
offer quality services to all stakeholders 
while observing high professional 
standards and respecting all relevant 
rules and regulations.
ii) Integrity:  The Board, Management 
and staff will always act with integrity 
while discharging the Bank’s mandate. 
We will remain transparent and 
accountable in all our operations.
iii) Innovativeness:   T h e B o a r d , 
Management and staff will nurture 
creativity and continued improvement 
in the processes of the Bank, leading to 
enhanced organizational performance, 
stakeholder interaction and customer 
experience.
iv) Diversity and Inclusiveness: The Board, 
Management and staff shall treat 
all people equally and fairly without 
discrimination and shall uphold the 
principles of gender equity, regional 
and ethnic balance. In the diversity of 
roles and professions in the Bank, we 
shall work as one team to deliver the 
Bank’s mandate in the most efficient 
and effective way.

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025
VIII
GOVERNOR’S MESSAGE
Kenya navigated 2025 amid a global 
environment characterized by moderating 
inflation, evolving trade dynamics, and 
persistent geopolitical tensions, particularly 
in the Middle East and Eastern Europe. 
Despite these external headwinds, the Kenyan 
economy remained resilient, supported by the 
strong performance of the services sector and 
a rebound in industrial activity. This positive 
trajectory is expected to continue into 2026, 
supported by exchange rate stability, strong 
performance of the services sector, the 
recovery of the industrial sector, and ongoing 
government reforms aimed at stimulating 
economic activity across key sectors.
The Kenyan banking sector registered improved 
financial strength in 2025, demonstrating 
stability and resilience. The sector’s total 
capital adequacy ratio stood at 20.7 percent 
in December 2025, above the minimum ratio 
of 14.5 percent. Similarly, the sector’s liquidity 
ratio remained above the statutory minimum 
level of 20 percent, averaging 59.3 percent. 
Total net assets registered an increase of 10.3 
percent from Ksh.7.57 trillion in December 
2024 to Ksh.8.35 trillion in December 2025. 
Customer deposits increased by 11.6 percent 
to Ksh.6.12 trillion in December 2025, from 
Ksh.5.48 trillion in December 2024.
On the regulatory front, a raft of regulatory 
reforms were undertaken in 2025 to ensure 
that the banking sector remained stable and 
resilient. In order to strengthen the banking 
sector liquidity and capital adequacy, the 
Central Bank of Kenya (CBK) issued guidelines 
on Liquidity Coverage Ratio (LCR), Net Stable 
Funding Ratio (NSFR) and Leverage Ratio (LR). 
Further, the Banking (Penalties) Regulations, 
2025, were gazetted pursuant to the Business 
Laws (Amendment) Act, 2024, to strengthen 
CBK’s supervisory f ramework, promote 
compliance, and enhance financial sector 
integrity. 
CBK has continued to advance reforms aimed 
at strengthening the resilience, transparency, 
and integrity of the banking sector through 
implementation of the Kenya Banking Sector 
Charter. Accordingly, in 2025, CBK revised the 
Risk-Based Credit Pricing Model (RBCPM) to 
anchor it to the Kenya Overnight Interbank 
Average Rate (KESONIA). This will enhance 
transmission of monetary policy and increase 
transparency and competition.
Taking cognizance of the existential threat 
of climate change as a significant socioeconomic concern, in April 2025, CBK issued 
the Climate Risk Disclosure Framework (CRDF) 
and the Kenya Green Finance Taxonomy 
(KGFT). The KGFT and CRDF aim to serve as 
tools to enable market participants classify 
whether their specific economic activities are 
‘green’ or environmentally sustainable and 
promote the transition to a green economy. 
Following the greylisting of Kenya by the 
Financial Action Task Force (FATF) in February 
2024, Kenya made a high-level political 
commitment to work with the FATF to address 
the action items set out under the FATF Action 
Plan for Kenya. During 2025, CBK implemented 
a range of regulatory, supervisory and capacitybuilding initiatives. These were aimed at 
addressing the action items and strengthening 
the effectiveness of the country’s anti-money 
laundering, countering the f inancing of 
terrorism and countering proliferation 
financing (AML/CFT/CPF) framework. These 
initiatives included strengthening AML/CFT/
CPF supervisory capacity and issuance of 
guidance notes on Customer Due Diligence 
(CDD), Beneficial Ownership (BO), Politically 
Exposed Persons (PEPs), and the Physical 
Cross-Border Movement of Cash.
On the economic front, the global economy 
is expected to weaken amid heightened 
geopolitical tensions and the energy price 
shock associated with the conflict in the 

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025

GOVERNOR’S MESSAGE
Middle East. This will be offset by increased 
investment and demand in technology-related 
sectors, particularly artificial intelligence (AI). 
The outlook remains uneven across countries, 
depending on their exposure to energy price 
developments and their integration into the 
global technology value chain. Growth in 
Sub-Saharan Africa is expected to remain 
relatively stable, supported by gains from past 
macroeconomic stabilization and structural 
reform efforts across several countries. On 
the domestic f ront, economic growth is 
projected to remain above both global and 
sub-Saharan Af rica averages, supported 
by resilient domestic demand and robust 
performance in the services sector.
Against this backdrop, CBK’s vision remains 
one of a banking sector that is responsible, 
disciplined and aligned to customer needs. 
Accordingly, banks continue to operationalize 
the four pillars of the vision: customercentricity, risk-based pricing, transparency, 
and ethical culture. More broadly, banks must 
take a long-term view and scale up their 
contribution to the shared prosperity of the 
Kenyan citizenry.
Dr. Kamau Thugge, CBS
Governor, Central Bank of Kenya

X
FOREWORD BY DIRECTOR, BANK SUPERVISION
Kenya Banking Sector 
Kenya’s banking sector remained stable and 
resilient in 2025, characterized by sufficient 
capital and liquidity buffers. The total capital 
adequacy ratio stood at 20.7 percent in 
December 2025, well above the minimum 
adequacy ratio of 14.5 percent whereas liquidity 
stood above the minimum statutory level of 
20 percent at an average of 59.3 percent in the 
same period.
The key highlights of the sector’s financial 
performance were:-
•	 Total net assets registered an increase 
of 10.3 percent from Ksh.7.57 trillion in 
December 2024 to Ksh.8.35 trillion in 
December 2025. This is attributable to 
increase in government securities by 
Ksh.384.5 billion (18.2 percent), loans and 
advances by Ksh.240.5 billion (6.6 percent), 
balances at Central Bank by Ksh.188.9 
billion (61.0 percent), and cash by Ksh.3.7 
billion (3.8 percent).
•	 Customer deposits increased by 11.6 percent 
to Ksh.6.12 trillion in December 2025, 
from Ksh.5.48 trillion in December 2024. 
The increase in deposits was attributed 
to deposit mobilization initiatives by 
commercial banks.
•	 The profit before tax for the sector increased 
by Ksh.46.0 billion (17.7 percent) from 
Ksh.260.3 billion in December 2024, to 
Ksh.306.3 billion in December 2025. The 
increase in profitability was attributed to a 
higher decrease in total expenses (Ksh.67.4 
billion) compared to a decrease in total 
income (Ksh.21.5 billion).
•	 Gross loans increased by 6.8 percent from 
Ksh.4,070.2 million in December 2024, to 
Ksh.4,347.2 million in 2025. The increase 
in gross loans is attributed to increased 
demand for credit by the various economic 
sectors. 
•	 The average liquidity ratio as at December 
2025, increased to 59.3 percent from 56.0 
percent in December 2024. The increase 
in the liquidity ratio is mainly attributed 
to a higher growth in total liquid assets 
as compared to the growth in short-term 
liabilities. 
•	 The stock of Non-Performing Loans (NPLs) 
decreased by 0.1 percent to Ksh.696.9 billion 
in December 2025 from Ksh.697.3 billion 
in December 2024. Asset quality, which 
is measured by the ratio of gross NPLs to 
gross loans improved with gross NPLs to 
gross loans ratio decreasing to 16.0 percent 
in December 2025 from 17.1 percent in 
December 2024. The improvement in asset 
quality was mainly due to repayments, 
recoveries and increased new advances.
Regulatory Reforms 
On the regulatory front, a raft of regulatory 
reforms were undertaken in 2025 to ensure 
that the banking sector remained stable and 
resilient. The key reforms include:-
•	 In order to strengthen the banking sector 
liquidity and capital adequacy, the Central 
Bank of Kenya (CBK) issued guidelines on 
Liquidity Coverage Ratio (LCR), Net Stable 
Funding Ratio (NSFR) and Leverage Ratio 
(LR) on April 17, 2025. These guidelines are 
intended to enhance the sector’s liquidity 
and capital frameworks, promoting greater 
financial stability and resilience across the 
industry.
o The LCR guideline ensures banks 
maintain enough High-Quality Liquid 
Assets (HQLA) to withstand a 30-day 
liquidity stress scenario. 
o The NSFR promotes stable, long-term 

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025
XI
FOREWORD BY DIRECTOR, BANK SUPERVISION
funding by requiring banks to match 
long-term assets with stable funding 
sources.
o The LR ensures banks hold sufficient 
core capital relative to total exposures, 
limiting excessive leverage.
•	 Advancing climate-risk and sustainable 
finance initiatives through the issuance 
of the Climate Risk Disclosure Framework 
(CRDF) and Kenya Green Finance Taxonomy
(KGFT) in April 2025. 
o C R D F a d v a n c e s t r a n s p a r e n c y 
and accountability in climaterelated financial risk management, 
aligning the banking sector with 
global standards including the 
International Sustainability Standards 
Board (ISSB), International Financial 
Reporting Standard S2 (Climaterelated Disclosures) IFRS S2 and Basel 
Committee principles. The framework 
is structured around four pillars 
namely: Governance, Strategy, Risk 
Management, and Metrics and 
Targets. 
o The KGFT provides a common 
f r a m e w o r k f o r c l a s s i f y i n g 
environmentally sustainable economic 
activities, enabling consistent and 
credible identification, assessment, and 
disclosure of green finance. It defines 
eligible activities, establishes technical 
screening criteria and environmental 
and social safeguards, and supports 
financial institutions in measuring, 
reporting, and mobilising green finance 
across priority sectors.
•	 Introduction of a revised Risk-Based 
Credit Pricing Model anchored on the 
Kenya Shilling Overnight Interbank 
Average Rate (KESONIA). The new model 
is expected to enhance monetary policy 
transmission, improve transparency in 
lending rates, and promote risk-sensitive 
pricing of credit. By the end of the year, 
all banks had obtained Board approval for 
their pricing frameworks and commenced 
implementation of the new model.
•	 Issuance of the Banking (Penalties) 
Regulations, 2025. Amendments to 
section 55(3) of the Banking Act under 
the Business Laws (Amendment) Act, 
2024 strengthened the legal framework 
governing penalties in the banking sector. 
CBK subsequently issued the Banking 
(Penalties) Regulations, 2025, to give effect 
to the amendments and facilitate their 
implementation. 
The Banking (Penalties) Regulations, 2025, 
introduced a standardized framework 
for the assessment and imposition of 
penalties on institutions and their senior 
management for violations of the Banking 
Act. The Regulations are designed to 
promote consistency in enforcement, 
strengthen accountability, and foster a 
culture of compliance across the banking 
sector.
•	 Strengthening the effectiveness of the 
Anti-Money Laundering, Countering The 
Financing of Terrorism and Countering 
Proliferation Financing (AML/CFT/CPF) 
framework - Following the grey listing of 
Kenya by the Financial Action Task Force 
(FATF) in February 2024, Kenya committed 
to an FATF-led action plan to address the 
gaps set out under the FATF Action Plan for 
Kenya. During the year, CBK implemented 
a range of regulatory, supervisory and 
capacity-building initiatives aimed at 
addressing these action items. These 
measures include: -
i. S t re n g t h e n i n g A M L /C F T/C PF 
supervisory capacity of staff and 

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025
XII
FOREWORD BY DIRECTOR, BANK SUPERVISION
financial resources for AML/CFT/
CPF supervision. These measures 
strengthened supervisory capacity 
and improved oversight of financial 
institutions under CBK’s supervisory 
purview.
ii. The enactment of the Virtual Asset 
Service Providers Act, 2025, which 
establishes a legislative framework 
for the licensing and regulation 
of the activities of Virtual Asset 
Service Providers (VASPs), and 
designated CBK and the Capital 
Markets Authority (CMA) as the 
regulatory authorities responsible, 
among other functions, for licensing 
VASPs as defined under the Act. The 
Act provides for supervisory powers 
of the regulators for AML/CFT/CPF 
purposes, including the authority to 
undertake inspections, investigations 
and enforcement action.
iii. Issuance of the following Guidance 
Notes on Preventive Measures 
to suppor t the AML /CFT/CPF 
compliance framework:-
•	 Guidance Note on Customer 
Due Diligence (CDD) outlines the 
core requirements for identifying 
and verifying customers using 
re l i a b l e a n d i n d e p e n d e n t 
sources. It emphasizes the 
importance of adopting a riskbased approach to determine the 
extent of CDD measures, tailored 
to the nature and risk profile of 
each customer. Additionally, the 
guidance sets out obligations for 
ongoing monitoring of customer 
relationships, enabling financial 
institutions to detect suspicious 
activity and ensure that customer 
information remains current 
t h r o u g h o u t t h e b u s i n e s s 
relationship.
•	 Guidance Note on Politically 
Exposed Persons (PEPs)
addresses the identification of 
individuals who are, or have been, 
entrusted with prominent public 
functions, including their family 
members and close associates. 
It outlines the need for enhanced 
due diligence (EDD) to mitigate 
the higher risks associated with 
PEPs, particularly in relation to 
the source of funds and wealth. 
It also provides for ongoing 
monitoring of accounts and 
transactions involving PEPs to 
ensure timely detection and 
reporting of unusual or suspicious 
activity. 
•	 Guidance Note on Beneficial 
Ownership (BO) provides 
practical direction on identifying 
individuals who ultimately 
own or control a legal person 
or arrangement. It details 
procedures for verifying the 
identity of benef icial owners 
using credible and independent 
sources and sets expectations for 
maintaining accurate and up-todate records. The Guidance Note 
also recognises the challenges 
institutions face in accessing 
reliable BO data and underscores 
the importance of recordkeeping practices that support 
transparency and effective risk 
management.
•	 Guidance on Cross-Border 
Movement of Physical Cash 
which provides a risk-based 
f r a m e wo r k to a s s i s t C B K

XIII
FOREWORD BY DIRECTOR, BANK SUPERVISION
regulated financial institutions 
i n c o m p l y i n g w i t h l e g a l 
requirements governing crossborder movement of physical cash, 
while strengthening measures 
to detect and deter money 
laundering, terrorist financing, 
proliferation financing, and other 
illicit f inancial flows through 
effective monitoring, reporting, 
and inter-agency coordination. 
This supports compliance with 
FATF Recommendation 32 
(Cash Couriers), which requires 
countries to establish effective 
systems to detect, restrain, 
and investigate suspicious 
cross-border movements of 
currency and bearer negotiable 
instruments.
iv. Enhance the understanding of 
preventive measures by financial 
institutions (FIs), through outreach 
programmes, which includes 
increasing the filing of Suspicious 
Transaction Reports (STRs) and 
promptly implementing Targeted 
Financial Sanctions (TFS).
•	 The sector witnessed the following new 
entrants and exits: -
o B a n k A l - H a b i b L td ( B A H L ) o f 
Pakistan voluntarily closed down its 
Representative Office in Kenya effective 
May 15, 2025, following a strategic 
decision by BAHL to rationalize 
its foreign operations. BAHL was 
authorized by CBK to establish a 
Representative Office in Kenya on April 
9, 2018. Its exit brought the number of 
authorized Representative Offices in 
Kenya from 10 to 9. 
o In 2025, 110 Digital Credit Providers 
(DCPs) were licensed: 41 in June 27 in 
September and 42 in December. This 
brings the total number of licenced 
DCPs to 195 from the 85 DCPs licensed 
in 2024.
2026 Outlook
Looking ahead, the banking sector is expected 
to remain resilient, supported by a stable 
macroeconomic environment, continued 
digitalization, enhanced credit market 
efficiency, and ongoing regulatory reforms. 
However, institutions must remain vigilant 
to emerging risks, including cyber threats, 
operational disruptions, climate-related risks, 
and evolving global financial conditions. The 
Central Bank of Kenya will continue to pursue 
a forward-looking, risk-based, and technology 
enabled supervisory approach to safeguard 
financial stability and promote a safe, sound, 
inclusive, and innovative financial sector. 
Notable initiatives in the coming year include:-
a) Comprehensive review of CBK’s Prudential 
Guidelines (PG’s) and Risk Management 
Guidelines (RMG’s). The exercise is aimed 
at updating the PGs and RMGs to suit 
the banking industry’s current operating 
environment and to be reflective of current 
market realities.
b) E n h a n c e m e n t o f t h e D o m e s t i c 
Systemically Important Banks (D-SIBs) 
framework. This is supervisory framework 
is intended to strengthen the identification, 
assessment, and supervision of institutions 
whose distress or failure could have a 
material adverse impact on the stability of 
Kenya’s financial system and the economy. 
The enhanced framework will incorporate 
risk-based supervisory measures and 
additional prudential requirements 
commensurate with the systemic 
importance of designated institutions, 
consistent with international standards and 
domestic financial stability objectives.

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025
XIV
c) Move towards near-real-time supervision
through the collection of supervisory data on 
an almost real-time basis. The Central Bank 
of Kenya (CBK) will continue implementing 
the Granular Data Integration (GDI) project, 
a strategic supervisory technology initiative 
aimed at transforming the regulatory 
reporting framework. The project will 
integrate CBK’s Enterprise Data Warehouse 
with the systems of Supervised Financial 
Institutions (SFIs), enabling the collection, 
storage, and reporting of granular data. 
Once implemented, the integration will 
strengthen CBK’s off-site surveillance 
capabilities, enhance the targeting and 
effectiveness of on-site inspections, and 
support near-real-time monitoring of 
financial institutions, thereby improving 
supervisory responsiveness and risk 
identification.
MATU MUGO
DIRECTOR, BANK SUPERVISION 
DEPARTMENT
FOREWORD BY DIRECTOR, BANK SUPERVISION

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025
XV
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
“A Year of Continued Uncertainty.” 2025 
witnessed easing inflationary pressures in 
advanced economies. Conversely, geopolitical 
tensions, trade policy uncertainties, and 
climate-related shocks continued to escalate.
The global and regional economies: Global 
economic growth is estimated to have grown 
by 3.5 percent in 2025 and is estimated to 
weaken to 3.0 percent in 2026. In Sub-Saharan 
Africa (SSA), economic growth in 2025 was 
estimated at 4.5 percent. SSA growth is 
projected to remain relatively stable at 4.3 
percent in 2026.
The domestic economy recorded growth 
in 2025 at 4.6 percent in 2025, compared to 
4.7 percent in a similar period in 2024. The 
marginal decrease was mainly on account 
of slower growth of agriculture and services 
sectors, which outweighed the recovery in 
industrial sector. 
Strong performance of the banking 
sector: The banking sector posted a strong 
performance in a challenging environment 
to December 2025. Against this backdrop, 
the sector’s total capital adequacy ratio stood 
at 20.7 percent in December 2025, above 
the minimum ratio of 14.5 percent. Similarly, 
the sector’s liquidity ratio remained above 
the statutory minimum level of 20 percent, 
averaging 59.3 percent. Total net assets 
registered a significant increase of 10.3 percent 
from Ksh.7.57 trillion in December 2024, to 
Ksh.8.35 trillion in December 2025. Customer 
deposits increased by 11.6 percent to Ksh.6.12 
trillion in December 2025, from Ksh.5.48 trillion 
in December 2024.
This report highlights the structure of 
the Kenyan banking sector, supervisory 
developments, macroeconomic conditions, 
banking sector performance, regional and 
international development initiatives. The 
banking sector is projected to remain resilient 
in 2026, as banks continue working for and 
with Kenyans.

(DCPs) and 88 foreign exchange (forex) 
bureaus. Out of the 39 banking institutions, 
37 were privately owned while the Kenya

Bank Supervision Department
Bank Supervision Department (BSD)’s 
mandate as stipulated under section 4(2) of 
the Central Bank of Kenya (CBK) Amendment 
Act, 2026 is to foster the liquidity, solvency and 
proper functioning of a stable market-based 
financial system. The following are the main 
functions of BSD: -
i. Development of legal and regulatory 
frameworks to foster stability, efficiency 
and access to f inancial services. The 
Department achieves this objective 
through: -
•	 Continuous review of the Banking Act, 
Microfinance Act, Building Societies 
Act, Regulations and Guidelines 
issued thereunder, which lay the legal 
foundation for banking institutions, 
non-bank financial institutions, deposit 
taking microfinance institutions and 
building societies.
•	 Continuous review of Regulations 
and Guidelines for Foreign Exchange 
Bureaus, Digital Credit Providers, 
Money Remittance Providers, Credit 
Reference Bureaus and Mortgage 
Refinance Company licensed under the 
Central Bank of Kenya Act.
ii. Processing licenses of Commercial Banks, 
Non-Bank Financial Institutions, Mortgage 
Finance Institutions, Mortgage Refinance 
Companies, Representative Offices, NonOperating Holding Companies, Foreign 
Exchange Bureaus, Microfinance Banks, 
Credit Reference Bureaus, Digital Credit 
Providers and Money Remittance Providers.
iii. Conducting onsite evaluation of the 
financial condition and compliance with 
statutory and prudential requirements of 
institutions licensed under the Banking Act, 
Microfinance Act; and Foreign Exchange 
Bureaus, Money Remittance Providers, 
Digital Credit Providers and Mortgage 
Refinance Companies licensed under the 
Central Bank of Kenya Act.
iv. Conducting offsite surveillance of 
institutions licensed under the Banking Act, 
Microfinance Act, and Foreign Exchange 
Bureaus, Digital Credit Providers, Money 
Remittance Providers and Mortgage 
Refinance Companies licensed under the 
Central Bank of Kenya Act through the 
receipt and analysis of returns received 
periodically. The Department also processes 
corporate approvals for banking institutions 
regarding opening and closing of places of 
business, the appointment of directors 
and senior managers, appointment of 
external auditors, the introduction of new 
products/services, increase of bank charges 
and review of annual license renewal 
applications in accordance with statutory 
and prudential requirements.
v. BSD is a member of the National Task 
Force on Anti-Money Laundering and 
Combating the Financing of Terrorism and 
Financing the Proliferation of Weapons 
of Mass Destruction (NTF). The NTF’s 
primary mandate is to develop a legal 
and regulatory anti-money laundering, 
combating the financing of terrorism 
and proliferation financing (AML/CFT/
CPF) framework that safeguards Kenya’s 
financial system from being exploited for 
money laundering, terrorism financing, 
and proliferation financing. The National 
Treasury chairs the NTF, while the Financial 
Reporting Centre (FRC) serves as the 
Secretariat. 
As CBK’s representative to the NTF, 
BSD participates in the initiatives of 
the Eastern and Southern African AntiMoney Laundering Group (ESAAMLG), 

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025

STRUCTURE OF THE BANKING SECTOR

one of the nine regional bodies affiliated 
to the Financial Action Taskforce (FATF) 
the international standard setter on 
AML/CFT/CPF measures. Membership in 
ESAAMLG currently comprises 21 countries 
in Eastern and Southern Africa. Its principal 
mandate is to assist member countries 
to develop effective AML/CFT/CPF legal 
and regulatory frameworks aligned to 
international standards.
vi. Participation in regional activities organized 
by regional and international bodies or

and Southern Africa (MEFMI) and African 
Rural and Agricultural Credit Association 
(AFRACA).
vii. Facilitation of the signing of Memoranda

authorities.

1.2 Ownership and Asset Base of Commercial Banks
The total net assets in the banking sector stood at Ksh.8.35 trillion as at December 31, 2025, compared 
to Ksh.7.57 trillion as at December 2024, recording an increase of 10.3 percent. There were 20 
operating local private commercial banks and 2 operating local public commercial banks, which 
accounted for 70.6 percent and 0.5 percent of total net assets respectively. A total of 17 operating 
commercial banks were foreign owned and accounted for 28.9 percent of the sector’s assets as 
indicated in Table 1 and Chart 3.

STRUCTURE OF THE BANKING SECTOR
1.3 Distribution of Commercial Banks 
Branches
The number of bank branches increased 
from 1,573 in 2024, to 1,611 in 2025, which 
translates to an increase of 38 branches (2.4 
percent). Nairobi County registered the highest 
increase in the number of branches by 7 
branches (Appendix XVI). A total of 20 counties 
registered an increase of 42 bank branches 
while 4 counties registered a decrease of 4 
bank branches. In 23 counties, there was no 
change in bank branches. The increase in bank 
branches is mainly attributed to opening of 
new branches by some commercial banks in 
emerging growth areas. 
1.4 Commercial Banks Market Share 
Analysis
The Kenyan commercial banks are classified 
into three peer groups using a weighted 
composite index. The index comprises net 
assets, total deposits, capital and reserves, 
number of deposit accounts and number 
of loan accounts. A bank with a weighted 
composite index of 5 percent and above is 
classified as a large bank. A medium bank 
has a weighted composite index of between 
1 percent and 5 percent while a small bank 
has a weighted composite index of less than 
1 percent.
For the year ended December 31, 2025, there 
were 8 large banks with a combined market 
share of 69.7 percent, 11 medium banks with a 
combined market share of 23.2 percent and 20 
small banks with a combined market share of 
7.1 percent as shown in Table 2, Chart 4 and 
Appendix V.

STRUCTURE OF THE BANKING SECTOR
In 2025, the banking sector capital and reserves 
increased by 18.6 percent to Ksh.1,403.8 billion 
in December 2025, from Ksh.1,183.6 billion in 
December 2024. Large, Medium and Small 
peer groups registered increased capital and 
reserves. The increase in capital and reserves is 
attributable to additional capital injections by 
commercial banks as well as retained earnings 
from the profits realized in the year. 
The banking sector registered an increase 
in profitability in 2025, with profit before tax 
increasing by Ksh.46.0 billion (17.7 percent) 
from Ksh.260.3 billion in December 2024, 
to Ksh.306.3 billion in December 2025. The 
increase in profitability was attributed to a 
higher decrease in total expenses (Ksh.67.4 
billion) compared to a decrease in total income 
(Ksh.21.5 billion).
The Large peer group accounted for 78.8 
percent of the total pre-tax profit in 2025, a 
decrease from 89.2 percent recorded in 2024. 
The Small peer group proportion of total pretax profit increased from 0.5 percent in 2024, 
to 2.3 percent in 2025. The Medium peer group 
proportion of total pre-tax profit increased 
from 10.4 percent in 2024, to 19.0 percent in 
2025.
Total deposits increased by 9.9 percent to 
Ksh.6.3 trillion in December 2025, from Ksh.5.8 
trillion in December 2024.
1.5 Automated Teller Machines (ATMs)
The number of Automated Teller Machines 
(ATMs) decreased by 61 (2.66 percent) to 2,228 
in December 2025, from 2,289 in December 
2024, as indicated in Table 3. The decrease 
in ATMs in the banking industry in 2025 was 
driven by:
•	 t h e d e s i re to e n h a n ce c u s to m e r 
convenience and accessibility to banking 
services via mobile/digital channels, 
especially for those in remote areas or with 
busy schedules;
•	 increased competition; and,
•	 the need for cost-effective solutions.

STRUCTURE OF THE BANKING SECTOR
1.6 Asset Base of Microfinance Banks
As of December 31, 2025, the number of 
licensed microfinance banks remained steady 
at fourteen (14). Among these, one (1) held a 
community microfinance bank license, while 
thirteen (13) held nationwide microfinance 
bank licenses.
The overall f inancial position of the 
microf inance banks f rom 2024 to 2025, 
was characterized by notable decreases in 
net advances and borrowings. The sector 
experienced a 4.1 percent decline in total assets 
in 2025, with total assets standing at Ksh.55.5 
billion, compared to Ksh.57.9 billion in 2024.
As highlighted in Table 4, lending remained 
the primary activity for micro-finance banks, 
with the net loan portfolio accounting for 53 
percent of their total assets. The net advances 
decreased by 6.1 percent, from Ksh.31.2 billion 
in 2024 to Ksh.29.3 billion in December 2025. 
This decline in loans was attributed to a 
strategic decision to reduce lending to manage 
non-performing loans, loans sale off to other 
lenders, and heightened competition from 
other credit providers. Customer deposits 
and borrowings were the primary sources of 
funding, comprising 81 percent and 8 percent 
of the microfinance banks’ total funding, 
respectively.
Customer deposits increased by 4.9 percent, 
from Ksh.43.0 billion in 2024 to Ksh.45.1 billion 
in 2025. Further, borrowings significantly 
decreased from Ksh.5,723 million, accounting 
for 10 percent of total liabilities and equity 
in 2024, to Ksh.4,409 million, representing 8 
percent of total liabilities and equity in 2025, 
indicating a growing shift from borrowing as 
a funding source.

STRUCTURE OF THE BANKING SECTOR
The large peer group registered an increase 
in market share, rising from 86.2 percent as at 
December 31, 2024, to 88.2 percent in 2025. The 
increase was primarily driven by the downward 
migration of KWFT and Sumac MFBs from the 
large peer group. Consequently, the market 
share of the small peer group shrunk from 
negative 0.9 percent in 2024, to negative 30.7 
percent as at December 31, 2025.
Faulu MFB maintained its leadership position 
in the sector, with changes in market shares 
across institutions largely driven by a mix of 
reported financial losses, business expansions, 
and capital injections from strategic investors.
1.8 Distribution of Foreign Exchange 
Bureaus
As at December 31, 2025, the number of forex 
bureaus increased to eighty-eight (88) from 
eighty-one (81) at December 31, 2024, following 
the licensing of eleven (11) forex bureaus and 
conversion of four (4) bureaus to money

DEVELOPMENTS IN THE BANKING SECTOR
2.1 Introduction
The banking sector posted a strong 
performance in 2025, in the face of both 
favourable trends and adverse developments 
globally and domestically. Globally, the 
operating environment continued being 
impacted negatively by geopolitical tensions, 
with both the Russia-Ukraine and IsraeliPalestinian conflicts in progress. These 
continued to disrupt global supply chains 
particularly in the energy sector. Domestically, 
economic confidence was boosted by a stable 
macroeconomic environment reflected in low 
inflation and stability in the exchange rate, the 
recovery of the industrial sector, and sustained 
policy interventions aimed at easing access 
to credit. Notwithstanding these, a perceived 
high cost of doing business posed challenges 
to the sector. 
The microfinance banking sector reported a 
17.4 percent decline of cumulative pre-tax loss 
from Ksh.3.5 billion in 2024 to Ksh.2.9 billion in 
2025. Six microfinance banks (MFBs) reported 
profits, up from four MFBs in 2024, signalling 
recovery in performance.
For the banking sector, 2025 was particularly 
notable for implementation of several reforms, 
including a phased increment in minimum 
capital requirements for commercial banks, 
implementation of CBK’s Risk-Based Credit 
Pricing Model (RBCPM), as well as issuance 
of the Banking (Penalties) Regulations, 2025.
The Guidelines on Liquidity Coverage Ratio 
(LCR), Net Stable Funding Ratio (NSFR) 
and Leverage Ratio (LR) were also issued to 
strengthen capital and liquidity, and ultimately 
resilience of the banking sector. Further, 
the enactment of the Virtual Asset Service 
Providers Act (No.20 of 2025) brought virtual 
asset service providers under regulation of CBK 
and the Capital Markets Authority.
Additional reforms covered the issuance of the 
Climate Risk Disclosure Framework (CRDF) and 
the Kenya Green Finance Taxonomy (KGFT), as 
well as continued efforts to strengthen the 
AML/CFT/CPF framework to address Kenya’s 
greylisting by the Financial Action Task Force 
(FATF) early in 2024. 
2.2 Banking Sector Charter 
CBK issued the Kenya Banking Sector Charter 
(KBSC) in 2019, following the repeal of the 
capping on commercial banks’ lending rates 
in November 2019. The Charter represents 
a commitment f rom institutions in the 
banking sector to entrench a responsible and 
disciplined banking sector cognizant of, and 
responsive to, the unique socio-economic 
realities of the Kenyan populace. The Charter is 
premised on the banking sector’s vision hinged 
on four central pillars: adoption of customercentric business models, risk-based pricing, 
enhanced transparency and information 
disclosure, and entrenching an ethical culture.
All institutions submitted their plans as 
required and also engaged closely with CBK on 
their implementation of the Charter. To a large 
extent, the institutions have integrated into 
their strategy and operations all the 4 pillars 
of the banking sector charter. 
Banks started implementing the Risk-Based 
Credit Pricing Model (RBCPM), which is one 
of the pillars of the KBSC, in 2022, following 
extensive engagements between CBK and the 
financial institutions from 2019. During these 
engagements, banks embarked on developing 
and refining their credit risk-based pricing 
models that would deliver on the overall 
objective of implementing differential credit 
pricing based on a customer’s credit rating. 
While the model had largely achieved its 
objectives in embedding the principles of 
RBCPM in credit pricing, some of the banks 

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 15
DEVELOPMENTS IN THE BANKING SECTOR
had not implemented their RBCPMs for pricing 
of customers’ credit as envisioned. Additionally, 
some of the models’ output results were very 
high and unrealistic, an indicator that most 
models were not suitable for effectively pricing 
loans.
On August 26, 2025, CBK issued a revised 
RBCPM. The model provided that the lending 
rate for variable rate loans will be ‘The Kenya 
Overnight Interbank Average Rate (KESONIA) 
+ Premium (“K”)’+ Fees/Charges. The use of 
KESONIA as the common reference rate was 
decided since it is market-based. Further, 
the overnight interbank average rate closely 
aligns with the policy rate (Central Bank Rate 
(CBR)) under the current monetary policy 
implementation f ramework. The model 
provides that where the use of KESONIA as 
the common reference rate is not practical, 
customers may be availed the use of CBR as 
the alternative reference rate. 
Where institutions opted to adopt KESONIA, 
they are required to use KESONIA compounded 
in arrears rate as the common reference 
rate. The premium component (‘K’) may 
comprise the bank’s lending‑related operating 
costs, the return to shareholders, and the 
borrower‑specific risk premium. Further, fees 
and charges levied separately to customers 
may include loan origination, arrangement, 
commitment, default, and late payment fees. 
However, this must be fully disclosed to both 
customers and CBK. 
The revised RBCPM took effect from September 
1, 2025, for all new variable-rate loans. As for 
existing variable rate loans, the revised RBCPM 
will take effect from February 28, 2026, at 
the end of a 6-month transition period for 
finalisation of the necessary arrangements, 
including obtaining board approval by 
November 30, 2025.
As at December 31, 2025, all banks had obtained 
approval from their respective Boards of 
Directors for their RBCP models, together with 
the corresponding policies and procedures.
2.3 Consolidation, Mergers and 
Acquisitions, and New Entrants
In 2025, no new mergers or partnerships 
emerged among commercial or microfinance 
banks. The Central Bank of Kenya (CBK) actively 
promoted new entrants to the banking sector 
to boost financial inclusion, competition, and 
innovation. During the year, CBK approved the 
following new participants:
i. Eleven forex bureaus to engage in forex 
bureau business, and conversion of four 
(4) forex bureaus to money remittance 
providers, bringing the total number of 
forex bureaus to eighty-eight (88).
ii. Seven money remittance providers to 
engage in money remittance business. 
This brings the total number of money 
remittance providers to thirty-three (33). 
iii. On the non-banks side, CBK granted 
licenses to an additional one hundred and 
ten (110) Digital Credit Providers (DCPs) in 
2025, as follows:- 
o 41 DCPs in June 2025.
o 27 DCPs in September 2025. 
o 42 DCPs in December 2025. 
With an additional 110 DCPs, the total 
number of licensed DCPs as at December 
2025 stood at 195.
iv. The Central Bank of Kenya (CBK) announced 
the cancellation of the authority granted 
to Bank Al-Habib Ltd (BAHL) of Pakistan 
to operate BAHL Representative Office 
in Kenya, pursuant to Section 43 of the 

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 16
DEVELOPMENTS IN THE BANKING SECTOR
Banking Act. BAHL’s exit from Kenya, 
effective May 15, 2025, followed a strategic 
decision by BAHL to rationalize its foreign 
operations. BAHL was authorized by CBK to

of licenced Representative Offices in Kenya 
to 9 from 10. 
2.4 Developments in Information and 
Communication Technology
2.4.1 Enhanced Risk and Cybersecurity 
Frameworks
Financial institutions are prime targets for 
cybercriminals due to the high value of 
financial data and assets they manage. Threats 
have evolved in both sophistication and scale, 
ranging from phishing and malware attacks 
to advanced persistent threats (APTs) and 
ransomware. Additionally, the rise of digital 
ecosystems and third-party service providers 
has introduced supply chain vulnerabilities, 
further increasing systemic risk
Enhanced risk and cybersecurity frameworks 
for the banking sector focus on proactive threat 
mitigation, integrating Artificial Intelligence 
(AI) driven monitoring, and ensuring 
operational resilience through frameworks like 
National Institute of Standards and Technology 
(NIST) and International Organization for 
Standardization (ISO) 270012.
Effective cybersecurity frameworks in banking 
consist of the following interconnected 
components3,4:
2  International Organization for Standardization (2022). ISO/IEC 
27001: Information Security Management Systems.
3  National Institute of Standards and Technology (2018). 
Framework for Improving Critical Infrastructure Cybersecurity
4  https://www.bis.org/bcbs/publ/d573.pdf
•	 Governance and Risk Management
form the foundation, requiring strong 
leadership oversight, clearly def ined 
policies, and integration with enterprise risk 
management systems. Boards of directors 
and senior management are increasingly 
held accountable for cybersecurity strategy 
and implementation.
•	 Threat Intelligence and Monitoring
enable banks to proactively identify and 
respond to potential threats. Security 
Operations Centers (SOCs) use real-time 
data and advanced analytics to detect 
anomalies and prevent breaches.
•	 Identity and Access Management 
(IAM) ensures that only authorized 
individuals can access sensitive systems 
and data. Techniques such as multifactor authentication and privileged 
access controls are essential in preventing 
unauthorized access.
•	 Data Protection mechanisms, including 
encryption and data loss prevention tools, 
safeguard sensitive financial information. 
These controls are critical in maintaining 
customer trust and regulatory compliance.
•	 Incident Response and Recovery
capabilities ensure that banks can quickly 
contain and recover from cyber incidents. 
Regular testing through simulations and 
cyber drills enhances preparedness.
•	 Third-Party Risk Management addresses 
vulnerabilities arising from external vendors 
and partners, which are increasingly 
integrated into banking ecosystems.
Enhanced risk and cybersecurity frameworks 
are essential for the stability and sustainability 
of the banking sector. As cyber threats grow 
in sophistication, financial institutions must 
adopt proactive, intelligence-driven, and 

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025
17
DEVELOPMENTS IN THE BANKING SECTOR
resilient approaches to safeguard financial 
systems, protect customer trust, and ensure 
regulatory compliance. Institutions that 
prioritize cybersecurity as a strategic imperative 
will be better positioned to navigate the 
complexities of the digital financial landscape.
2.4.2 Cloud Computing Governance
Cloud computing has become a foundational 
technology in the banking sector, enabling 
institutions to enhance scalability, reduce 
infrastructure costs, and accelerate digital 
innovation. However, the adoption of cloud 
computing introduces significant risks related 
to data security, regulatory compliance, 
and operational control. As a result, cloud 
computing governance has emerged as 
a critical framework to ensure that cloud 
adoption aligns with business objectives, 
risk management strategies, and regulatory 
requirements.
Cloud computing governance refers to the set 
of policies, procedures, controls, and oversight 
mechanisms that guide how an organization 
adopts and manages cloud services. It ensures 
that cloud environments are secure, compliant, 
and aligned with enterprise goals5
.
Effective cloud governance frameworks consist 
of the following core components6:
•	 Governance Structure and Policies 
- Institutions must establish clear 
governance structures that define roles, 
responsibilities, and decision-making 
processes. Policies should cover cloud 
adoption, data management, and risk 
controls.
5  National Institute of Standards and Technology (2020). NIST 
Cloud Computing Security Reference Architecture. 
 International Organization for Standardization (2015). ISO/IEC 
27017: Code of Practice for Information Security Controls for 
Cloud Services
6  Financial Stability Board (2019). Third-Party Dependencies in 
Cloud Services
•	 Risk Management and Compliance - 
Risk assessments must be conducted 
regularly to identify vulnerabilities in cloud 
environments. Compliance with regulatory 
standards and internal policies must be 
continuously monitored.
•	 Data Governance and Protection - Data 
classif ication, encryption, and access 
controls are essential to protect sensitive 
financial information. Institutions must 
ensure that data is stored and processed 
in compliance with legal requirements.
•	 Vendor and Third-Party Management 
- Cloud services are typically provided by 
external vendors. Therefore, institutions 
must conduct due diligence, establish 
service-level agreements (SLAs), and 
continuously monitor vendor performance.
•	 Identity and Access Management (IAM) 
- Strong authentication and authorization 
mechanisms are required to ensure that 
only authorized users can access cloud 
resources.
•	 Monitoring and Auditing - Continuous 
monitoring and auditing of cloud 
environments help detect anomalies, 
ensure compliance, and support incident 
response.
•	 Incident Response and Business 
Continuity- Institutions must develop 
robust incident response plans and disaster 
recovery strategies to address potential 
disruptions in cloud services.
2.4.3 Data Analytics and Big Data
With the rise of digital banking, mobile 
transactions, and fintech ecosystems, financial 
institutions now handle massive volumes of 
structured and unstructured data. Financial 
institutions are leveraging data analytics and 

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 18
DEVELOPMENTS IN THE BANKING SECTOR
big data to improve decision-making, enhance 
customer experiences, and strengthen risk 
management7.
Key Applications of big data include:
•	 Fraud Detection and Security: Financial 
institutions use real-time analytics to detect 
f raudulent transactions and enhance 
security, minimizing losses.
•	 Customer Insights and Personalization: 
A n a l y z i n g c u s t o m e r b e h a v i o r 
(demographics, transaction history, 
interactions) allows financial institutions 
to offer tailored services, increasing loyalty.
•	 Risk Management and Credit Scoring: 
Advanced analytics, such as Random 
Forest algorithms8, improve loan default 
assessment and risk evaluation.
•	 Operational Efficiency: By processing data 
faster, banks streamline operations and 
improve productivity.
•	 Regulatory Compliance: Big data helps 
manage regulatory requirements and 
detect compliance breaches.
Despite its advantages, the adoption of 
big data in banking presents the following 
challenges:
•	 Data privacy and security. Banks must 
protect sensitive customer information and 
comply with data protection regulations 
•	 Data quality and integration. Inaccurate 
7  International Organization for Standardization (2018). ISO/IEC 
20546: Big Data Overview and Vocabulary.
National Institute of Standards and Technology (2019). Big

or incomplete data can lead to incorrect 
insights and poor decision-making. 
Integrating data from multiple sources, 
including legacy systems, can also be 
complex and resource-intensive.
•	 The shortage of skilled professionals
in data science and analytics is another 
significant barrier. Financial institutions 
need highly trained personnel to manage 
and analyze large datasets effectively.
•	 Additionally, the cost of implementation
can be high, as institutions must invest in 
infrastructure, software, and training.
As the volume and complexity of data continue 
to grow, financial institutions should invest in 
advanced technologies, skilled personnel, and 
robust governance frameworks to fully harness 
its potential.
2.4.4 Robotic Process Automation (RPA)
Traditional banking operations often rely on 
manual, repetitive processes that are timeconsuming and prone to human error. Robotic 
Process Automation (RPA) has emerged as 
a powerful solution to these challenges by 
enabling the automation of rule-based tasks 
using software robots.
Robotic Process Automation refers to the 
use of software tools that mimic human 
interactions with digital systems to execute 
business processes. This form of automation 
uses rule-based software to perform business 
process activities at a high-volume, freeing up 
human resources to prioritize more complex 
tasks.9
By integrating RPA into their operations, banks 
are transforming how they deliver services, 
manage risk, and remain competitive in a fastevolving financial landscape.
9  https://www.ibm.com/think/topics/rpa

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 19
DEVELOPMENTS IN THE BANKING SECTOR
Applications of RPA in the banking sector
•	 Customer onboarding and Know Your 
Customer (KYC) processes. Financial 
institutions are required to verify customer 
identities and comply with regulatory 
standards, which traditionally involves 
extensive manual data entr y and 
verification. RPA automates document 
collection, validation, and data input, 
reducing onboarding time.
•	 In loan processing, RPA streamlines tasks 
such as application data extraction, credit 
checks, and eligibility assessments. Bots10
can quickly gather information f rom 
multiple systems, process it, and generate 
reports, enabling faster decision-making 
and improved customer satisfaction.
•	 Fraud detection and compliance - 
RPA bots can monitor transactions, 
flag suspicious activities, and generate 
compliance reports. While RPA itself is rulebased, it often works alongside analytics 
and artif icial intelligence systems to 
enhance fraud detection capabilities.
•	 Account reconciliation, where it matches 
transactions across systems, identifies 
discrepancies, and produces accurate 
reports. This reduces the risk of financial 
errors and ensures transparency in financial 
records.
•	 Customer service operations by 
automating routine queries and service 
requests. When integrated with chatbots 
and AI tools, it enables faster response 
times and consistent service delivery.
Despite its advantages, RPA implementation 
is not without challenges. These include:
10  A bot is an automated software application that is programmed to do certain tasks.
•	 The need for process standardization. RPA 
works best with well-defined, rule-based 
processes, and poorly structured workflows 
can limit its effectiveness.
•	 Employees may resist automation due 
to concerns about job displacement, 
requiring banks to invest in training and 
reskilling initiatives.
•	 There are also security and governance 
risks associated with RPA. Since bots often 
handle sensitive financial data, banks 
must implement strict access controls and 
monitoring mechanisms. Compliance with 
regulations such as the Data Protection 
Act is essential to ensure data privacy and 
protection.
By automating repetitive and time-consuming 
tasks, RPA allows financial institutions to 
reduce costs, improve compliance, and deliver 
enhanced customer experiences11.
2.4.5 CBK Artif icial Intelligence (AI) 
Hackathon 2025
CBK held an Artif icial Intelligence (AI) 
Hackathon on November 20, 2025. The theme 
of the Hackathon was “Ethical and Responsible 
AI in the Financial Sector”.
The theme of ethical and responsible AI/ML 
initiatives in the financial sector challenged 
fintechs to provide solutions on the risks and 
challenges of AI. The theme aimed to address 
the need to ensure that as institutions leverage 
AI/ML for the benefit of customers and efficient 
service delivery, they also prioritize ethical 
and responsible use of the technology. This is 
important, given that the use of AI has brought 
about concerns on biased data and models, 
explainability of ML models, data privacy, and 
governance issues.

2.5 Mobile Phone Financial Services
Mobile financial services in Kenya continued 
to expand in 2025, with mobile subscriptions 
rising by 10 percent to 78.4 million in December 
2025 from 71.4 million in 2024, representing a 
penetration rate of 149.5 percent. This growth 
was driven by increased mobile phone device 
penetration, with the number of devices rising 
by 6.2 million from 72.1 million in 2024 to 78.4 
million as at December 2025.
Despite this expansion, mobile money activity 
slowed over the same period. The volume of 
transactions declined by 30 percent, from 
309.3 million in 2024 to 217.6 million in 2025, 
while the total value of transactions fell more 
modestly by 4 percent, from Ksh.753.5 billion to 
Ksh.722.5 billion. Transaction volumes reached 
their lowest point in February 2025, marking 
the weakest level since April 2023. In contrast, 
the sector’s agent network continued to grow, 
expanding by 24 percent from 381,116 agents 
in 2024 to 473,536 in 2025. This sustained 
expansion reflects continued investment in 
access infrastructure and has strengthened 
financial inclusion, particularly in rural and 
underserved areas, where mobile money 
remains central to daily economic activity.
The decline in cash-in and cash-out 
transactions largely reflects shifting usage 
patterns rather than reduced relevance of 
mobile money. There was reduced frequency of 
low-value transactions, while users increasingly 
consolidated transfers into fewer, higher-value 
payments. At the same time, the growing 
adoption of alternative digital payment 
channels, including merchant payments 
and mobile banking platforms, has reduced 
reliance on agent-based cash conversion, 
signaling a gradual transition toward a more 
digitized payments ecosystem. This shift 
highlights evolving consumer behavior, with 
users increasingly leveraging digital channels 
for both personal and commercial payments.
Overall, these trends reflect a broader 
transformation in Kenya’s mobile money 
ecosystem: agents remain important for 
financial access, but direct digital transactions 
are increasingly becoming dominant, 
reshaping liquidity flows and the role of agents 
in the economy.
2.5.1 Summary of MFS Transactions Data
Against the backdrop of accelerating digital 
uptake, widespread reliance on mobile phones, 
and increasing demand for accessible financial 
solutions, mobile financial services in Kenya

2.6 New Products
CBK continued to approve new banking 
products and related charges as provided for 
under Section 44 of the Banking Act, which 
provides that no banking institution can 
increase its rate of banking or other charges 
except with the prior approval of the Minister. 
The Cabinet Secretary of the National Treasury 
delegated this role to the Governor of the 
Central Bank of Kenya via Legal Notice 34 of 
May 2006 on the Banking (Increase of Rate 
of Banking and Other Charges) Regulations, 
2006. 
While processing such applications, CBK 
considers: 
• Whether the proposed increase conforms 
with the Government’s policy of establishing 
a market-oriented economy in Kenya. 
• The average underlying inflation rate 
prevailing over twelve months preceding 
the application. 
• Whether the newly proposed charges 
are justifiable and are comparable to the 
industrial average. 
• Whether risks surrounding the new 
products have been identified and the 
mitigations have been put in place. 
• Whether the new product aligns with 
existing laws and regulations relating to 
consumer protection and Anti-Money 
Laundering / Combating the Financing of 
Terrorism (AML/CFT).
The f inancial services industry is being 
restructured by the everchanging consumer 
needs, innovative f inancial products, 
technological advancement, and the use 
of multiple delivery channels. To remain 
competitive in the new landscape, banks 
have continued to introduce new products, 
expand the existing ones, and add new delivery 
channels. Banks strive to enhance access 
to customers as well as differentiate their 
products and services by use of alternative 
delivery channels such as electronic-banking, 
mobile-banking, and recently, cloud-based 
channels. 
In the year 2025, the Central Bank of Kenya 
noted the introduction of 36 new products in 
the market and approved the related charges. 
2.6.1 Technology and employee efficiency
In 2025, a bank employee was on average 
handling 1,962 deposit accounts whereas in 
2024, an employee was handling 2,941 deposit 
accounts (Table 9). The decline in efficiency 
is explained by the decline in the number of 
deposit account holders as compared to the 
increase in the number of staff. The number 
of deposit account holders declined by 29.4 
percent. The decline was due to consolidation 
of a financial inclusion fund savings balances 
into a single custody-administered account, 
resulting in aggregated reporting of customer 
wallet balances. Additionally, there was an 
increase in the recruitment of new staff within 
the period.

2.7 Transition to the Risk Based Credit Pricing Model
a) Background
Kenya is taking a major step forward in reforming its banking 
sector through the Risk-Based Credit Pricing Framework. This 
framework was developed jointly by the Central Bank of Kenya 
(CBK) and the Kenya Bankers Association and is designed to 
make lending more transparent, fair, and stable, while also 
strengthening the effectiveness of monetary policy.
b) Risk-Based Pricing Overview
Kenya’s credit pricing framework has evolved significantly over 
the past decade. The repeal of statutory interest rate caps in 
2019 restored banks’ discretion in credit pricing and facilitated 
the adoption of risk-based lending. However, the post-repeal 
period was characterised by variation in lending rates across the 
banking sector. Changes in the Monetary Policy Committee’s 
pronouncements on the 
Central Bank Rate (CBR) were 
also not uniformly or effectively 
reflected in lending rates.
To address this, in April 2025, the 
CBK proposed a standardised 
f ramework that would link 
loan pricing to the policy rate, 
Central Bank Rate (CBR), as 
the base reference rate plus a 
bank-specific premium (“K”).
Following extensive industry 
consultation, the framework 
was refined in August 2025 
to use Kenya’s  Risk-Free Rate 
(RFR), off icially known as 
the Kenya Shilling Overnight 
Interbank Average (KESONIA). 
KESONIA is a transactionbased overnight interbank 
rate used as the benchmark 
for local-currency variable-rate 
loans. 
Where KESONIA is not practical, 
customers may be availed 
the use of the Central Bank 
Rate (CBR) as the alternative 
reference rate. KESONIA closely 
aligns with the policy rate, CBR, 
under the current monetary 
p o l i c y i m p l e m e n t a t i o n 
framework.
The new framework took effect 
on September 1, 2025, with a 
six-month transition period 
for existing facilities ending on 
February 28, 2026.
Under the new system, lending 
rates will be calculated as 
follows:

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 25
DEVELOPMENTS IN THE BANKING SECTOR
The total lending rate = KESONIA + Premium 
(“K”) + Fees and Charges
Whereby:
•	 Premium includes the costs related to 
lending, return to shareholders, and the 
credit risk profile of the borrower. 
•	 Fees and charges include origination, 
processing, negotiation and commitment 
fees.
To ensure transparency, banks are required to 
publish on their websites and on the Total Cost 
of Credit (TCC) website their weighted average 
lending rates, weighted average premium (K), 
and fees and charges for each of their lending 
products. 
c) Systemic Benefits
The introduction of a risk-based approach 
to credit pricing offers multiple benefits for 
Kenya’s banking sector and economy:
•	 Enhanced Transmission of Monetary 
Policy Decisions: Linking lending rates 
to KESONIA ensures that changes in the 
CBK monetary policy rates are reflected in 
lending rates in a timely and transparent 
manner. 
•	 Interest Rates Based on Customers’ Credit 
Risk: Borrowers are charged interest rates 
that reflect their individual credit risk 
profiles rather than applying similar rates 
to all customers. Lower-risk customers 
benefit from lower borrowing costs, which 
encourages borrowers to maintain a good 
credit history.
•	 Greater Pricing Transparency: The use 
of a common benchmark rate, together 
with clear disclosure of the bank’s base 
rate and credit risk premium, makes it 
easier for customers to understand how 
their loan interest rate is determined. In 
addition, disclosure of lending rates and 
other credit costs through the Total Cost of 
Credit (TCC) website will enable borrowers 
to compare loan offers across banks and 
make informed borrowing decisions.
•	 Data-Driven Lending: The f ramework 
promotes the use of robust credit scoring 
models, high-quality data, and advanced 
analytics to enhance risk assessment, 
improve pricing accuracy, and strengthen 
overall credit decision-making.
Kenya’s transition to a KESONIA anchored 
Risk-Based Credit Pricing Model represents a 
significant milestone in the modernisation of 
the country’s banking framework. This reform 
establishes a transparent, fair, and risk-based 
approach to credit pricing.
Over time, this reform is expected to enhance 
the effectiveness of monetary policy, 
strengthen the functioning of the financial 
sector, and support a more efficient allocation 
of credit to the most productive sectors of the 
Kenyan economy.
2.8 Innovative Micro, Small, and Medium 
Enterprises (MSME) Products by 
Banks
A review of the MSME products offered in the 
year 2025, in the banking sector reveal that 
the Kenya Commercial Banking sector has 
increasingly focused on developing innovative 
products and services tailored to the needs 
of MSMEs. MSMEs are the backbone of most 
economies, contributing signif icantly to 
employment and GDP. These innovations aim 
to address the unique challenges MSMEs face, 
such as limited access to credit and the need 
for efficient financial management tools. The 
products have assisted MSMEs in managing 
business volatility as well as providing funds 
for working capital, business expansion, 
business protection and yield enhancement, 
with flexible repayment options suiting the 
businesses. 

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 26
DEVELOPMENTS IN THE BANKING SECTOR
Banks are leveraging on technology to address 
the unique needs of MSMEs, which are rapidly 
being transformed. These include: 
•	 Advanced digital ecosystems: Adopting 
modular, API-driven platforms that 
seamlessly integrate with MSMEs existing 
ERP, accounting, payroll, and inventory 
management systems. These integrations 
enable real-time data sharing, automated 
reconciliations, and streamlined cashflow management. Through rich analytics 
and customized dashboards, MSMEs gain 
actionable insights into sales performance, 
working capital cycles, and credit utilization, 
supporting better decision-making and 
financial planning. 
•	 Usability: Banks are deploying globally 
scalable digital platforms that support 
multi-currency transactions, crossborder payments, and compliance with 
international standards. These platforms 
are complemented by 24/7 digital support 
channels, including AI-powered chatbots 
and remote relationship management, 
ensuring consistent service availability 
regardless of geography or time zone. 
•	 Cost-efficient digital delivery methods: 
MSMEs are benef itting f rom lower 
transaction fees, affordable credit 
assessment models powered by alternative 
data, and simplified onboarding processes. 
These efficiencies allow banks to offer 
cheaper, faster, and more inclusive financial 
services, especially for underserved and 
early-stage enterprises.
•	 Accessibility: Banks are leveraging mobile 
and web-based platforms to provide 
MSMEs with easy, secure, and convenient 
access to f inancial services. Features 
such as digital account opening, instant 
payments, online loan applications, and 
real-time account monitoring ensure that 
MSMEs can manage their finances anytime 
and anywhere, even in remote or informal 
market settings. 
•	 Capacity building: Beyond f inancial 
products, banks are embedding capacitybuilding tools within their digital platforms 
to strengthen MSMEs business and 
financial management capabilities. These 
include digital financial literacy modules, 
cash-flow forecasting tools, bookkeeping 
support, and advisory content tailored to 
different business life cycles.
2.9 Operations of Representative 
Off ices of Authorized Foreign 
Financial Institutions in Kenya
Representative offices in Kenya are established 
by foreign banks for purposes of marketing 
their products and services in the country. They 
act as a liaison between their parent institutions 
and their clients in Kenya. Representative 
Offices in Kenya are authorized and regulated 
by the Central Bank of Kenya under section 
43 of the Banking Act (Cap 488) and CBK 
Prudential Guideline on Authorization of 
Representative Offices in Kenya CBK/PG/17. 
Representative Offices are expressly prohibited 
from undertaking primary banking business14
as defined in the Banking Act. Representative 
Offices are permitted to undertake marketing 
or liaison roles on behalf of their parent and 
affiliated entities. 
The primary motivation for establishing 
Representative Offices in foreign jurisdictions 
is to enable parent banks to expand their 
business activities and enhance their 
global footprint. Representative Off ices 
14  Banking business means accepting from members of the 
public; money on deposit repayable on demand or at the 
expiry of a fixed period or after notice; money on current 
account and payment on and acceptance of cheques; and the 
employing of money held on deposit or on current account, 
or any part of the money, by lending, investment or in any 
other manner for the account and at the risk of the person so 
employing the money.

serve as a tangible presence 
in international markets, 
providing valuable market 
insight, extending outreach, 
nurture relationships, ensuring 
regulatory compliance, and 
facilitating business growth.
Gathering local intelligence 
enables Representative Offices 
to customize their products and 
services to meet the unique 
needs of clients in different 
regions, ultimately fostering 
business opportunities and 
revenue growth. Additionally, 
they serve as key information 
hubs for their parent banks, 
providing insights into market 
trends, opportunities, and risks. 
This in turn empowers parent 
banks to make well-informed 
decisions regarding their 
global strategy and operations.

2.10 2025 Surveys:
2.10.1 Residential Mortgage Survey 2025
CBK conducts an annual mortgage survey to monitor 
developments and challenges in the mortgage market for 
residential housing. A detailed questionnaire was distributed to 
all commercial banks to collect data for the year ending 2025. 
The information collected comprised:
a) Size of mortgage portfolio.
b) Mortgage loan characteristics. 
c) Constraints to mortgage market financing.
d) Mortgage risk characteristics.
e) Mortgage refinancing.
f) Mortgage outlook for 2026.
15  Exchange rate: 1 USD=Ksh.129 as at December 31, 2025

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 28
DEVELOPMENTS IN THE BANKING SECTOR
Below are the highlights of the Residential 
Mortgage Survey as at December 31, 2025.
a) Size of Mortgage Portfolio
•	 The value of mortgage loans outstanding 
was Ksh.307.2 billion in December 2025, as 
compared to Ksh.279.3 billion in December 
2024 (Appendix XIII). This represents an 
increase of Ksh.27.9 billion or 10.0 percent. 
The increase was due to new mortgage 
loans granted in 2025.
•	 90.6 percent of lending to the mortgage 
market was by 9 institutions. That is 7 banks 
from the large peer group (77.4 percent) 
and two from the medium sized banks (13.2 
percent) in December 2025, as compared 
to 89.9 percent of lending by 9 institutions 
in 2024, 8 banks from the large peer group 
(81.6 percent) and one from the medium 
sized banks (8.3 percent).
•	 The outstanding value of non-performing 
mortgage loans increased from Ksh.46.0 
billion in December 2024, to Ksh.50.2 billion 
in December 2025. The non-performing 
mortgage loans to gross mortgage loans 
ratio was 16.3 percent in December 2025, 
as compared to 16.5 percent in December 
2024. The ratio was above the industry 
gross NPLs to gross loans ratio of 16.0 
percent in December 2025, and below the 
industry gross NPLs to gross loans ratio of 
17.1 percent in December 2024.
•	 There were 30,762 mortgage loans in the 
market in December 2025, up from 30,016 
in December 2024. This was an increase 
of 746 mortgages or 2.5 percent. This was 
mainly due to new mortgage loans granted 
in the year.
•	 The average mortgage loan size increased 
from Ksh.9.0 million in 2024, to Ksh.10.0 
million in 2025. This was mainly due to 
higher values of mortgage loans advanced 
in the year.
b) Mortgage Loan Characteristics
•	 The interest rates for mortgage loans 
in 2025 ranged from 7.5 percent to 19.6 
percent compared to 8.2 percent to 20.4 
percent in 2024. The average interest rate 
on mortgages was 13.5 percent in 2025, as 
compared to 15.2 percent in 2024
•	 About 75.6 percent of mortgage loans 
were on variable interest rates in 2025, as 
compared to 85.9 percent in 2024. This 
was due to increase in mortgage loans 
under fixed interest rates from 14.1 percent 
in December 2024 to 24.3 percent in 
December 2025.
•	 Loan to value (maximum loan as a 
percentage of property value) was pegged 
below 90 percent of the property value by 
majority of banks in 2025 and 2024.
•	 The average loan maturity was 11.5 years with 
a minimum of 5.7 years and a maximum of 
18 years in 2025, as compared to an average 
loan maturity of 11.1 years with a minimum 
of 5.3 years and a maximum of 18 years 
in 2024. This is an indication that banks 
increased maturity period of mortgage 
facilities in 2025.

Institutions suggested a number of measures

mortgage market in Kenya. Some of the 
suggested measures include: -

building low-cost housing projects.
• Finalization of the digitalization of the land 
registry processes to support the mortgage 
documentation process. 
•	 Reduction of stamp duty and transaction 
costs for first-time home buyers.
•	 Increase the focus on implementation 
of affordable housing programs by the 
government. 
•	 Availability of more low-cost housing units.
•	 Expansion of affordable housing projects.
•	 Availability of affordable long-term funds 
through initiatives such as the Kenya 
Mortgage Refinance Company (KMRC).
•	 Improvement of land administration and 
titling systems.
•	 Improvement of basic inf rastructure 
services to developers by national and 
county governments to enable access by 
prospective buyers.
•	 Streamlining and simplifying of the 
legal and regulatory process governing 
the mortgage sector, for transparency, 
efficiency and certainty.

•	 N a t i o nw i d e m o r tg a g e e d u c a t i o n

•	 S t a n d a r d i z a t i o n o f m o r t g a g e 
documentation to facilitate faster resale
