e) Mortgage Risk Characteristics
Institutions indicated the following as the main 
risk factors that are examined more closely 
before a mortgage loan to a household is 
approved: -
•	 Credit Rating of the borrower to ascertain 
credit soundness.
•	 Genuineness of security documents 
presented.
•	 Ability to repay the mortgage loan.
•	 Sustainability of source of funds. 
•	 Borrowers’ repayment capability both in 
the short run and long run.
•	 For salaried individuals, the nature and 
duration in employment.
•	 Age of the borrower.
•	 Borrower’s character based on the credit 
history and Credit scores.
•	 Ratio of monthly loan instalment to 
disposable income.
•	 Loan to value ratio.
•	 Ease of property disposal in the event of 
default.
•	 Value, type and location of property. 
The main risk factors examined more closely by 
financial institutions before a mortgage loan 
to a business is approved include: -
•	 Credit Rating of the borrower to ascertain 
credit soundness.
•	 Ability to pay from the available cash flows 
generated by business operations.
•	 Financial repayment capacity of the 
borrower.
•	 The organizational structure of the business 
and its corporate governance.
•	 Property use status- residential or 
commercial.
•	 Type of the business and business risk 
associated.
•	 Financial performance of the business.
•	 Current business turnovers.
•	 The ability of the business to generate 
sufficient income to guarantee repayment 
of the loan.
•	 Past loan repayment records of the 
company directors.
•	 Future projections of business income.
•	 Duration in which the business has been 
in operation.
•	 Experience of the business proprietor 
in the respective field and the quality of 
management.
•	 Industry performance.
•	 Geographical location of the property in 
question (urban or rural).
•	 Legal ownership structure of the business.
f) Mortgage Refinancing
The survey indicated that ten institutions had 
been advanced mortgage refinancing from the 
Kenya Mortgage Refinance Company (KMRC) 
in 2025, compared to seven institutions in 2024. 
The ten institutions had outstanding facilities 
amounting to Ksh.19.6 billion in December 
2025 as compared to seven institutions with 
outstanding facilities amounting to Ksh.11.9 
billion in December 2024.
g) Mortgage Outlook for 2026
The mortgage market is expected to remain 
stable and the demand for mortgage loans is 
expected to increase owing to:
•	 Stabilization of interest rates.
•	 Increased supply of affordable housing 
units through the government backed 
affordable housing projects.

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 31
•	 Faster turnaround in closure of land 
transactions due to the ongoing 
digitization of processes at the Ministry 
of Lands.
•	 Availability of discounted long-term 
financing from institutions like KMRC and 
partnership of developers with financiers 
to provide affordable housing.
2.10.2 Innovation Survey 2025 
In February 2026, CBK conducted an Innovation 
Survey, which was aimed at collecting present 
and forward-looking information on Fintech 
developments in the Kenyan financial sector in 
the year ended December 31, 2025. The survey 
collected data on the state of innovation from 
37 commercial banks, 1 mortgage finance 
institution, and 14 Microfinance Banks (MFBs). 
The survey was a follow-up to the 2018, 2019, 
2020, 2021, 2022, 2023, and 2024 Innovation 
Surveys. The 2025 Innovation Survey was 
undertaken against the backdrop of the 
resilience of the banking sector and enhanced 
technological growth in data science and 
analytics globally. Below is a summary of the 
survey results.
i) Business Strategies: According to their 
business strategies, 71 percent of the 
institutions considered themselves as 
“better banks”, 17 percent as “distributed 
banks” and 8 percent as “new banks”.16 In 
16  Better Bank – An institution seeks to become a ‘better bank’ 
by leveraging on enabling financial technologies (Fintech) 
to digitize and modernize its operations and business 
practices. Its market knowledge and Fintech investment 
will significantly improve its banking services and products 
offering.
New Bank – An institution seeks to become a ‘new bank’ by 
creating a ‘built for digital’ banking platform. The institution 
shall apply advanced Fintech to provide banking services, 
minimize operational costs, improve customer experience, 
and market their products through social media. 
Distributed Bank – An institution seeks to become a 
‘distributed bank’ through collaboration and partnership with 
Fintech start-ups. The institution seeks to compete for the 
ownership of the customer relationship by providing niche 
banking services. Such joint ventures will allow consumers to 
use multiple financial service providers, through a ‘plug and 
play’ digital interface. 
the 2024 Innovation Survey, 75 percent of 
the respondents considered themselves 
as “better banks”, 11 percent as “distributed 
banks” and 10 percent as “new banks”. 
Accordingly, the Kenyan banking 
institutions are increasingly evolving 
towards better banking in order to offer a 
wide array of services through leveraging 
f intechs to digitize and modernise 
operations and business practices.
ii) Innovation Units: The survey noted 
that 67 percent of the respondents had 
a dedicated function that spearheaded 
innovation activities compared to 65 
percent in the 2024 Innovation Survey. 
The slight increase in institutions with 
a dedicated innovation function was 
attributed to a slight increase in dedicated 
innovation teams in commercial banks 
from 68 percent in 2024 to 74 percent 
in 2025. This growth is largely attributed 
to the increasing need for banks to keep 
pace with rapid innovation and evolving 
market demands. Most banks noted that 
the main role of the innovation function 
is to modernize banking platforms, 
enhance customer experience through 
digital channels and improve operational 
efficiency. On average, innovation function 
teams constitute 60 percent male and 40 
percent female staff.
iii) Innovation Priorities: Credit, deposit, 
and capital-raising services remained 
the functional area where most MFBs 
introduced an innovative product in the 
period January 1 to December 31, 2025, 
with 64 percent of MFBs innovating in 
this area. While payments, clearing and 
settlement services was the functional

provide and manage direct customer relationships through 
‘frontend’ digital platforms. The institution will be relegated 
to offering commoditized banking functions such as 
deposit-taking, lending, and risk management, to the digital 
platforms that own and manage the customer relationships.

(app or USSD) for banking and customer relationship services, 35 percent of the banks noted 
that credit business remained the least digitized area of their operations, similar to the findings 
of the 2021, 2022, 2023, and 2024 surveys.

iv) Technological Developments: Application 
Programming Interfaces (APIs) have 
been adopted by most banks with 84 
percent and 79 percent adoption rate by 
commercial banks and MFBs respectively. 
This was followed by Cloud Computing at 
45 percent adoption rate, Big Data and 
Data Analytics at 45 percent adoption 
rate, and Biometrics Technology with 
an adoption rate of 45 percent across all 
banks.
v) Innovation Risks: Given the increased 
innovation in the banking sector, and 
reliance on APIs, Big Data, Data Analytics, 
and cloud computing, cyber-risk turned 
out to be the key risk area for institutions 
in their innovation endeavours. Of the 
institutions surveyed, 82 percent of 
commercial banks and 93 percent of 
MFBs identified it as the number one 
innovation-related risk. Cyber risk was 
also the top risk identified in the 2018-
2024 Innovation Surveys. A further 
analysis indicated that operational risk 
was identified as the second highest key 
risk area by 61 percent of commercial 
banks and 57 percent of MFBs, a shift 
from focus on compliance risks which 
was the second highest risk for banks in 
2024. The emphasis on operational risk in 
both commercial and microfinance banks 
stems from the growing digitalization, 
interconnectedness, and rapid evolution 
of the banking sector.
vi) Public Support, Policy and Regulation: 
Banks indicated that the top three forms 
of public support based on the 2025 survey 
were: direct funding support (58 percent), 
provision of infrastructure and services 
(56 percent),) and demand-side support 
(54 percent). The banks identified digital 
innovation, cyber security and data privacy, 
and fintech-friendly regulations as the 
policy focus areas that regulatory agencies 
need to address.
vii) Climate Action and Sustainability: Of the 
banks surveyed, 61 percent of commercial 
banks and 36 percent of microfinance 
banks indicated that they had innovated or 
are in the process of innovating a climate 
change related product. The constant 
trend in commercial banks is indicative of 
a constant awareness and commitment 
within the financial sector to address 
environmental challenges. Commercial 
banks cited the CBK Guidance on ClimateRelated Risk Management issued in 2021, 
as well as the Draft Climate Risk Disclosure 
Framework for the Banking Sector issued 
in 2024 as influential in their sustainability 
practices. Some of the climate changerelated initiatives included Environmental, 
Social and Governance (ESG) frameworks, 
green financing for renewable energy and 
climate-smart solutions, and partnerships 
for sustainable practices and programmes 
such as environmental conservation.
viii) CBK Innovative Initiatives: CBK conducts 
Innovative Initiatives to bring African 
institutions closer together to cooperate 
on FinTech innovation by providing a 
platform for connections, collaborations, 
and exchange of ideas. Survey respondents 
proposed several solutions that could push 
the industry forward, which could be 
presented at the CBK Innovative Initiatives 
forum, 2026, some the key solutions 
include: -
•	 Formation of an annual banking 
innovation roundtable.
•	 Establishment of a Shariah-Compliant 
FinTech Innovation Hub.
•	 Cross-border case studies.
•	 Regulatory Sandbox.

•	 Thought Leadership on Emerging 
Technologies and Finance Models.
•	 Regulatory Technology (RegTech).
•	 Development of an industry-Led 
fintech accelerator and innovation lab.
•	 Financial literacy programs to educate 
consumers on digital banking and 
responsible finance.
•	 Digital financial inclusion and MSME 
support.
•	 Open Finance and Banking as a 
Service (BaaS).
ix) Conclusion
The banking sector is leveraging 
big data and analytics, application 
programming interfaces (APIs), artificial 
intelligence (AI), biometrics, and cloud 
computing to improve service delivery 
through data-driven insights. This is 
consistent with the 2024 Innovation 
Survey findings.
API technology and cloud computing 
offer support to banking sector services 
in their innovative use of data. Cloud 
computing is increasingly becoming 
important due to the efficiencies of 
scale and cost savings. 
The banking sector noted the potential 
opportunities of virtual assets in 
enhancing f inancial access to the 
unbanked by providing alternative 
payment and investment channels, 
improving transaction speed, and 
reducing transaction costs. Conversely, 
they highlighted that virtual assets bear 
risks such as challenges in AML and CFT 
controls, cybersecurity risks, fraud, and 
high volatility among others.
The banking sector is also cognizant of 
the importance of addressing climate 
action and has continued with its efforts 
towards offering innovative climaterelated financial products and services. In 
this regard, CBK’s Guidance on Climaterelated Risk Management, issued in 2021, 
and the Draft Climate Risk Disclosure 
Framework for the Banking Sector 
issued in 2024 were timely policy actions 
and which will steer the banking sector 
towards greening efforts. 
2.10.3 Artificial Intelligence Survey 2025
Artificial Intelligence (AI) technologies are 
increasingly being adopted across the Kenyan 
banking sector to improve operational 
eff iciency, customer service, and risk 
management. In recognition of the rising 
importance of AI, CBK conducted a survey 
on the use of AI in the banking sector. 
The survey was conducted in March 2025, 
targeting commercial banks, mortgage 
finance institutions, Credit Reference Bureaus 
(CRBs), Microfinance Banks (MFBs) and Digital 
Credit Providers (DCPs). The Survey sought 
to assess the adoption of AI among financial 
institutions, understand key benefits, risks, 
and mitigation strategies and gather insights 
for policy development. Key findings are 
summarized below:
i) Adoption: 50 percent of all surveyed 
institutions indicated they had adopted 
AI solutions. This comprised 66 percent 
of commercial banks, 57 percent of MFBs, 
and 43 percent of DCPs. All CRBs indicated 
non-adoption of AI in their operations. 
Most of the institutions which had not 
adopted AI were exploring the potential 
use of AI in their institutions.
ii) Strategy and Governance: 30 percent 
of institutions had formal AI strategies 
while 62 percent had data strategies and 
dedicated data/AI teams. Institutions with 
AI strategies cited use cases such as credit 
DEVELOPMENTS IN THE BANKING SECTOR

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 35
scoring, fraud detection, and customer 
support chatbots.
iii) Applications of AI in the Banking 
Sector: The top three applications of 
AI and ML in the banking sector were 
credit risk assessment (65 percent), 
cybersecurity (54 percent), and customer 
service (43 percent). Notably, 83 percent of 
institutions indicated that they were likely 
to adopt AI for credit risk assessment in 
future, and 82 percent for cybersecurity, 
customer service, and electronic Knowyour-Customer (e-KYC) solutions.
iv) Risk Management: Common risks 
identif ied included data quality, 
governance challenges, cybersecurity 
risks, and third-party dependencies.
v) Challenges : T h e c h a l l e n g e s i n 
implementing AI included limited AIskilled staff, high costs of AI, and data 
and governance regulatory compliance 
challenges.
vi) Impact: AI has enhanced operational 
efficiency, improved decision-making, and 
enabled new financial products.
vii) Recommendations from Institutions: 93 
percent of respondents recommended 
that CBK issues a comprehensive 
Guidance on AI, covering governance 
and compliance, risk management, and 
incident management and reporting.

applications of AI and ML in the banking sector.

2.10.4 Third-Pary Technology Survey 2025
CBK’s regulatory philosophy towards innovation 
seeks to maximize the opportunities arising 
from innovation while minimizing risks. CBK 
continues to encourage the banking sector 
to innovate and ensure that the products 
prioritize customer needs while putting 
in place appropriate consumer protection 
safeguards.
An emerging trend in the banking sector 
is the adoption of Third-Party Technology 
Service Providers (TSPs) in the development 
or implementation of innovative products. 
TSPs enable the banking sector to increase 
revenues, reduce costs, improve operational 
resilience and quickly scale products and 
services.
T S P s s u p p o r t t h e m a n a g e m e n t o f 
IT inf rastructure, including network 
administration and server maintenance. 
They support the development of custom 
software applications specific to a bank’s 
requirements. Additionally, they provide cloudbased solutions for data storage, processing, 
and analytics, which offer scalability and 
flexibility.
Against this backdrop, CBK conducted a survey 
on TSPs for commercial banks and MFBs in 
March 2025. The survey aimed to provide a 
comprehensive analysis on the use of TSPs in 
the Kenyan banking sector, focusing on the 
current state, benefits, risks and future trends. 
The survey was geared towards providing 
valuable insights into how the banking sector 
can leverage third-party technology service 
providers to drive innovation while identifying 
any existing gaps and understanding future 
trends. The key survey findings are indicated 
below.
•	 All commercial banks and MFBs indicated 
that they relied on third-party TSPs in their 
operations.
•	 Commercial Banks use TSPs to support key 
functions. The most cited services include 
cloud-based services, mobile-banking and 
internet banking. Meanwhile, MFBs noted 
that they engage third parties to support 
both core banking and mobile banking.
•	 38 out of 52 respondents considered 
cybersecurity and data privacy concerns as 
the biggest challenges in engaging TSPs.
•	 83 percent of surveyed institutions reported 
having a formal incident response plan in 
place for breaches or failures involving 
third-party technology service providers. 
Meanwhile 15 percent indicated that such 
plans were under development while 2 
percent reported having no such plans in 
place.
•	 100 percent of commercial banks and 93 
percent of MFBs indicated that they had 
governance structures and frameworks for 
dealing with third parties. These typically 
included Third-Party Risk Management 
Committees, disclosure agreements, 
Service Level Agreements (SLAs), and 
incorporating third party vendor training 
into their governance frameworks with 
third-party TSPs. Majority of the institutions 
also required evidence of compliance with 
standards like ISO 27001,17 PCI-DSS,18 or ISO 
2230119 onboarding in their governance 
frameworks with TSPs.
17  ISO 27001 - an information security management standard 
that provides a structured framework for organizations to 
protect their information assets through the adoption of an 
information security management system.
18  PCI-DSS – a set of security standards designed to protect card 
transactions.
19  ISO 22301 – a security standard for business continuity 
management.
DEVELOPMENTS IN THE BANKING SECTOR

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 37
•	 89.5 percent of commercial banks had 
a clear process for incorporating lessons 
learned from incidents or audits into their 
third-party risk management frameworks. 
This indicated a strong focus on continuous 
improvement and structured risk 
management.
•	 89 percent of commercial banks and 57 
percent of MFBs responded that they 
had formal processes for identifying risks. 
Meanwhile approximately 12 percent 
of commercial banks and 43 percent 
of MFBs indicated that their processes 
for identifying third party risk was in 
development.
•	 79 percent of institutions surveyed reported 
using key performance indicators (KPIs) to 
monitor the performance of third party 
TSPs. Out of those, 61 percent of institutions 
surveyed reported the KPIs to the board.
•	 95 percent of commercial banks and 
86 percent of MFBs conducted regular 
internal audits on third party TSPs to 
identify gaps and ensure alignment with 
regulatory expectations.
•	 32 percent of commercial banks and 14 
percent of MFBs indicated that the current 
regulatory framework on outsourcing was 
supportive, while 7 percent of MFBs and 
3 percent of commercial banks stated 
that the current regulations acted as a 
barrier towards adoption of third parties. 
Additionally, 66 percent of commercial 
banks and 79 percent of MFBs noted that 
the existing regulations, while supportive, 
could be improved.

2.10.5 Survey on Guidance 
Note on Cybersecurity 
2017
The Central Bank of Kenya 
(CBK) issued a Guidance on 
Cybersecurity (Guidance) to 
commercial banks in August 
2017. The Guidance outlines 
the minimum requirements 
that institutions shall build 
upon in the development 
a n d i m p l e m e n t a t i o n o f 
strategies, policies, procedures 
and related activities aimed 
at mitigating cyber risk. In 
addition, the Guidance sets 
the minimum standards 
that institutions should 
adopt to develop effective 
cybersecurity governance and 
risk management frameworks.
Following the issuance of the 
Guidance, CBK conducted a 
survey in March 2025, on the 
adoption of the Guidance. 
The aim of the survey was to 
assess the level of cyber risk 
awareness, preparedness, and 
resilience within the financial 
sector following the issuance 
of the Guidance. All surveyed 
institutions indicated that they had a cybersecurity framework 
as depicted in Chart 8. Below is a summary of the survey results.
Chart 8: Institutions with a Cybersecurity Framework
i) All commercial banks had dedicated cybersecurity 
governance f rameworks, cybersecurity policies, and 
formalized business continuity management processes in 
place. The policies were reviewed and updated regularly to 
align with industry’s best practices, regulatory standards 
and emerging threats.
ii) 95 percent of commercial banks assessed and managed 
cybersecurity risks posed by third-party vendors, while 5 
percent did not. Institutions that did not assess and manage 
cybersecurity risks posed by third parties indicated that they 
vetted their vendors before onboarding them.
iii) 92 percent of commercial banks had Information Technology 
(IT) auditors within their internal audit teams, while 8 percent 
did not. Consequently, 97 percent of commercial banks 
conducted regular audits to evaluate the effectiveness of 
cybersecurity controls, while 3 percent did not.
iv) 100 percent of the respondents indicated that they had a 
process for reporting cybersecurity incidents. In addition,

Conclusion
Cybersecurity threats have continued to evolve 
and become more complex, with increased 
f requency of threats such as phishing, 
ransomware, Distributed Denial-of Service 
(DDoS) attacks, amongst others. Consequently, 
financial institutions are required to proactively 
secure their critical information assets to 
ensure that they remain resilient in the face 
of these persistent threats. The prevalence of 
the use of emerging technology by financial 
institutions to deliver services to customers has 
also increased their attack surface. Commercial 
banks have highlighted emerging technology 
areas that CBK should consider incorporating 
into the Guidance on Cybersecurity. 
As cyber threats evolve in scale and 
sophistication, updated guidance from Central 
Banks plays a critical role in safeguarding the 
stability, trust, and integrity of the financial 
system. Accordingly, CBK has embarked on the 
process of updating the 2017 CBK Guidance on 
Cybersecurity to commercial banks.
2.11 Employment Trends in the Banking 
Sector
The banking sector staff levels increased by 
2,284 (5.9 percent) from 38,840 in December 
2024, to 41,124 in December 2025 (Table 12). 
Clerical, and Secretarial and other staff cadres 
increased by 2,588 and 223 respectively. 
Medium peer group banks had the largest 
increase in the total number of staff.

2.12 Future Outlook
The Central Bank of Kenya (CBK) is dedicated to advancing financial stability, inclusion, digital 
innovation, service excellence, and integrity. Through progressive regulatory reforms, technological 
innovation, and the strategic use of data, CBK is committed to fostering a resilient, inclusive, and 
future-ready financial ecosystem that promotes sustainable economic growth and prosperity.

MACROECONOMIC CONDITIONS AND BANKING SECTOR PERFORMANCE
3.1 Global Economic Conditions
Global growth remained resilient in 2025, 
reflecting easing trade-related policy, 
accommodative f inancial conditions, 
accommodative monetary policy conditions, 
and continued technology-related business 
investment and productivity. However, 
according to the IMF July 2026 World Economic 
Outlook (WEO) update global growth is 
projected to weaken from 3.5 percent in 2025 
to 3.0 percent in 2026 and improve to 3.4 
percent in 2027. The subdued global outlook 
reflects the adverse effects of heightened 
geopolitical tensions and the energy price 
shock associated with the conflict in the Middle 
East, which are partly offset by increased 
investment and demand in technology-related 
sectors, particularly artificial intelligence (AI). 
The outlook for global growth continues to be 
dampened by geopolitical risks emanating 
from the deepening crisis in Iran and the 
broader Middle East, tensions in Northern 
Europe, the Russia-Ukraine conflict & Latin 
America, and trade policy uncertainty 
associated with higher effective tariffs.
In advanced economies, growth improved 
further in 2025, largely attributable to rising 
demand, recovering real incomes, and 
sustained household consumption following 
the end of the federal government shutdown, 
and the associated carryover in the U.S. 
Growth in the region is projected to moderate 
marginally from 1.9 percent in 2025 to 1.7 
percent in 2026 and remain at similar levels 
at 1.8 percent in 2027, reflecting divergent 
economic performance across countries amid 
the effects of the energy shock and heightened 
uncertainty associated with the conflict in the 
Middle East. In the United States, growth is 
projected at 2.3 percent in 2026 and 2.2 percent 
in 2027, supported by resilient domestic 
demand, fiscal policy support, investment in 
technology-related sectors, and the lagged 
effects of monetary policy easing in 2025, 
although rising trade barriers are expected 
to weigh on economic activity. In the Euro 
Area, growth is projected at 0.9 percent in 
2026 and 1.2 percent in 2027, reflecting weaker 
confidence, tighter financial conditions, and 
higher energy prices, particularly in Germany. 
In the United Kingdom, growth is projected to 
slow to 1.0 percent in 2026 before recovering 
to 1.3 percent in 2027 as the adverse effects of 
the energy shock gradually dissipate.
Growth in emerging markets and developing 
economies rebounded strongly in 2025, driven 
by improving economic activity in key trading 
partners, stronger net exports, solid investment 
and lower effective U.S. tariff rates. China 
remained more resilient than anticipated, 
mostly supported by increased fiscal stimulus 
and increased shipments to non-U.S. markets. 
Growth in the region is expected to slow 
down from 4.5 percent in 2025 to 3.8 percent 
in 2026 before recovering to 4.5 percent in 
2027, reflecting the adverse effects of higher 
global energy prices, heightened geopolitical 
tensions, and weaker economic activity in 
some major economies, particularly in China. 
China’s growth is projected at 4.6 percent in 
2026, supported by stronger-than-expected 
economic activity in the first quarter, lower 
U.S. effective tariff rates on Chinese goods, and 
policy stimulus measures. Nevertheless, higher 
global energy prices, persistent economic 
uncertainty, and structural headwinds are 
expected to weigh on economic activity. 
India’s growth is projected to remain robust 
at 6.4 percent in 2026 and 6.7 percent in 
2027, underpinned by strong domestic 
demand, particularly private consumption, and 
sustained momentum in the services sector.
Global financial conditions eased in 2025 
supported by rising risk-on appetite. However, 
the risk of financial stress remained significant 
reflecting in part the economic policy in the 
U.S. Financing conditions have become 
more challenging for emerging markets and 
developing economies, resulting in heightened 

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 43
MACROECONOMIC CONDITIONS AND BANKING SECTOR PERFORMANCE
risks of capital outflows and higher borrowing 
costs. Sovereign bond yields have risen amid 
a reassessment of term premia, reflecting 
persistent inflationary pressures, rising fiscal 
spending requirements, and strong private 
investment demand. Sovereign debt issuance 
is expected to moderate in 2026, while 
sovereign spreads are likely to remain elevated 
owing to heightened geopolitical uncertainty 
and tighter global financing conditions.
Global inflation pressures intensified in the first 
half of 2026, reflecting higher energy prices 
associated with the conflict in the Middle 
East and persistent geopolitical uncertainty. 
Global headline inflation is projected to rise 
to 4.7 percent in 2026 and moderate to 3.9 
percent in 2027, on the assumption of a 
gradual de-escalation of the US-Israel-Iran 
conflict. Inflation in the advanced economies 
is projected to increase to 3.0 percent in 2026 
and converge towards central bank targets 
in 2027. In emerging market and developing 
economies, inflation is expected at 5.8 percent 
in 2026 before easing in 2027. Monetary policy 
remained broadly accommodative across 
advanced economies and emerging markets 
and developing economies during 2025 
and the first half of 2026. Central banks are 
expected to remain cautious as they assess the 
inflationary impact of the Middle East conflict, 
as a large and sustained energy price shock 
could delay further monetary policy easing 
or necessitate a reversal of the current easing 
cycle. 
Risks to the global growth outlook remain 
tilted to the downside, reflecting considerable 
uncertainty regarding the scale and duration 
of the conflict in the Middle East and its 
potential implications for global trade, 
commodity markets, and financial conditions. 
A prolonged disruption to energy supply 
routes, including the Strait of Hormuz, could 
result in sustained increases in oil prices, 
higher inflationary pressures, and weaker 
global economic activity. On the upside, global 
growth could be supported by stronger-thanexpected investment and productivity gains 
in technology-related sectors, lower effective 
tariff rates than previously assumed, and carryover from strong growth outturn in 2025.

3.2 Regional Economy
Growth accelerated in in sub-Saharan Africa 
in 2025, supported by macroeconomic 
stabilization and reform efforts in key 
economies. Economic growth in the region 
is projected to remain broadly stable at 
4.3 percent in 2026 compared with 4.5 
percent in 2025, supported by gains from past 
macroeconomic stabilization and structural 
reform efforts across several countries. 
However, oil-importing non-resource-intensive 
countries in the region are expected to be 
affected by higher energy prices arising from 
the conflict in the Middle East. Growth in 
Nigeria is expected to strengthen to 4.1 percent 
in 2026 and 4.3 percent in 2027, reflecting 
improvements in macroeconomic conditions 
and ongoing policy reforms. South Africa’s 
growth is projected to improve modestly to 
1.1 percent in 2026 and 1.3 percent in 2027, 
supported by easing domestic constraints 
and a gradual recovery in economic activity. 
Kenya’s growth is expected to remain above 
both global and sub-Saharan Africa averages, 
supported by resilient domestic demand and 
robust performance in the services sector.

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 45
3.3 Domestic Economy
Kenya’s economy grew by 4.6 percent in 2025, compared with 4.7 percent in 2024, as slower growth 
in agriculture and services offset the recovery in industry. Industrial sector growth rebounded to 4.7 
percent from 1.1 percent, supported by strong performance in construction, mining and quarrying, 
and electricity and water supply. Agriculture growth moderated to 3.1 percent in 2025 from 4.4 
percent in 2024 largely due to unfavourable weather conditions, while services growth slowed to 
5.3 percent from 6.0 percent but still remained the main driver of growth. In the first quarter of 
2026, economic growth strengthened to 5.3 percent from 4.9 percent in the corresponding quarter 
of 2025, supported by robust activity in the industrial and services sectors.

3.4 Inflation
Headline inflation remained below the 5 percent medium term target in 2025, largely reflecting 
muted demand pressures, impact of stable exchange rate, and relatively lower energy prices. 
However, it began picking up modestly from July 2025, reflecting the easing monetary stance 
and seasonal factors. 
Chart 9a: Developments in Headline Inflation (%)
Source: Kenya National Bureau of Statistics
Core Inflation
Core inflation remained relatively low and 
stable in 2025, reflecting easing of monetary 
policy stance, stable exchange rate, and 
seasonal factors (Chart 9b). Movements of core 
inflation during the year were mainly driven by 
prices of processed food items, even as prices 
of non-food items remained relatively stable. 
In January 2026, core inflation increased to 
2.2 percent from 2.0 percent in December 
2025, largely driven by higher prices of select 
processed food items, particularly maize 
products. 
Non-Core Inflation 
Non-core inflation remained elevated in 2025, 
mainly driven by seasonal factors (Chart 9b). 
The favourable weather conditions experienced 
in the March-May 2025 rainfall season led to 
increased supply of fresh vegetables which 
had a moderating effect on non-core inflation 
between April and June 2025. However, prices 
of both food and energy items picked up in the 
second half of 2025, resulting in increased noncore inflation from 6.2 percent in June 2025 to 
11.2 percent in December 2025. In January 2026, 
non-core inflation eased slightly to 10.3 percent, 
due to lower prices of food items particularly 
onions and tomatoes.

Inflation Outlook 
Headline inflation is expected to remain 
around the medium-term target in the nearterm, supported by prudent monetary policy, 
stability of the exchange rate, and favourable 
weather outlook in the food growing areas of 
the country. However, this outlook is subject 
to risks, including uncertainty in the global 
environment due to US politics and trade 
tariffs and geopolitical tensions. Domestically, 
the weather outlook remains uncertain, which 
could impact food supply.
3.5 Modernization of Monetary Policy 
Implementation Framework
The Central Bank of Kenya monetary policy 
implementation framework adopted in August 
2023 continues to strengthen monetary policy 
transmission. The framework grounded on 
inflation targeting introduced an interest rate 
corridor around the Central Bank Rate (CBR). 
The interest rate corridor was initially set at ± 
250 basis points around the CBR but has since 
been reduced progressively to ±150 basis points 
in 2024 and to ±75 basis points in April 2025. 
The MPC also approved an adjustment of the 
Discount Window rate from 300 basis points 
above the CBR to 75 basis points above the CBR, 
which is the upper bound of the corridor. Since 
the introduction of the interest rate corridor 
around the CBR, monetary policy operations 
have been strengthened, ensuring that the 
interbank rate/ Kenya Shilling Overnight 
Interbank Average (KESONIA), as the operating 
target, closely tracks the CBR, and improved 
monetary policy transmission. The overnight 
interbank rate was officially renamed KESONIA 
from September 2025 (Chart 10).
Source: Kenya National Bureau of Statistics

3.6 Exchange Rates
The Kenya Shilling exchange rate strengthened against major international and regional currencies 
during 2025, reflecting increased foreign exchange inflows, impact of monetary policy measures, 
effects of reforms in the foreign exchange market, and External Liability Management Operations 
of the Government in 2025. The Kenya Shilling exchanged at an average of KSh.129.3 in 2025, against 
the US dollar, from an average of KSh.134.8 in 2024 (Table 15).

3.7 Interest Rates
Interest rates declined throughout 2025, 
reflecting a gradual easing of the monetary 
policy stance and improved liquidity conditions 
in the money market. The Monetary Policy 
Committee (MPC) reduced the Central Bank 
Rate (CBR) further in 2025 cumulatively by 
225 basis points to 9.0 percent. The MPC 
actions were aimed at stimulating lending by 
banks to the private sector and supporting 
economic activity, while ensuring inflationary 
expectations remain firmly anchored, and the 
exchange rate remains stable.
The Kenya Shilling Overnight Interbank 
Average (KESONIA) decreased to 9.08 percent 
in December 2025 f rom 11.45 percent in 
December 2024 and remained within the 
prescribed interest rate corridor around the 
CBR. Similarly, the interest rates on Government 
securities declined, with the 91-day Treasury bill 
rate at 7.77 percent in December 2025 from 
10.32 percent in December 2024.
Commercial banks’ average lending rates 
declined to 14.82 percent in December 2025 
from 16.89 percent in December 2024. The 
deposit rate also declined to 7.13 percent 
in December 2025 f rom 10.45 percent in 
December 2024, reflecting easing cost of 
funds. As a result, the interest spread increased 
to an average of 7.08 percent in 2025 compared 
to 5.77 percent in 2024, partly reflecting higher 
adjustment of deposit rates compared to 
lending rates (Chart 11). The introduction of 
the revised Risk Based Credit Pricing Model, 
which will be fully operational by March 2026 
aims to close the spread between the average 
commercial banks’ lending rates and deposit 
rates by enhancing transparency in loan 
pricing.
Chart 11: Interest Rates (percent)

3.8 Current Account Balance
The current account deficit widened to 2.1 percent of GDP in 2025 
from 1.3 percent of GDP in 2024. This is as a result of a higher 
deficit in the goods and services account and a slowdown in the 
secondary income account.
Chart 12: Current Account Balance
3.9 Fiscal Developments
Total government revenues and grants amounted to KSh.1,506.3 
billion in the first half of FY 2025/26, representing an 8.8 percent 
increase from KSh.1,383.9 billion collected in a similar period in 
2024/25 (Table 16). Tax revenue was below its target set. Total 
expenditure and net lending increased by 3.5 percent during 
the first half of FY 2025/26 to KSh.2,024.4 billion from KSh.1,955.9 
billion over a similar period in FY 2024/25 largely on account 
of increases in recurrent and development expenditure which 
surpassed the decline in County transfers. Total expenditure and 
net lending were below target, reflecting underperformance in 
development spending and county transfers. However, recurrent 
expenditure was above target. Consequently, the Government 
budgetary operations resulted in a higher deficit of KSh.518.1 
billion (2.7 percent of GDP) on a cash basis compared to a deficit 
of KSh.446.9 billion (2.5 percent of GDP) incurred over the same 
period in FY 2024/25. The deficit was above the target of 2.3 
percent of GDP.

3.10 Performance of the Banking Sector
The banking sector recorded a strong 
performance in the year ended December 
2025, against a backdrop of a challenging 
business and operating environment. The 
banking sector registered an increase in 
profitability in 2025, with profit before tax 
increasing by Ksh.46.0 billion (17.7 percent) 
from Ksh.260.3 billion in December 2024, 
to Ksh.306.3 billion in December 2025. The 
increase in profitability was attributed to a 
higher decrease in total expenses (Ksh.67.4 
billion) compared to a decrease in total income 
(Ksh.21.5 billion). The decrease in total expenses 
was mainly attributed to decrease in interest 
expense and advances (25.1 percent).
3.11 Commercial Banks Balance Sheet 
Analysis
The banking sector registered increase in 
financial strength in 2025, with total net assets 
recording an increase of Ksh.781.8 billion (10.3 
percent) from Ksh.7,568.0 billion in December 
2024 to Ksh.8,349.7 billion in December 2025 
(Table 17). This is attributable to increase in 
government securities by Ksh.384.5 billion 
(18.2 percent), loans and advances by Ksh.240.5 
billion (6.6 percent), balances at Central Bank 
by Ksh.188.9 billion (61.0 percent), and cash by 
Ksh.3.7 billion (3.8 percent).
Net loans and advances, government securities 
and other assets accounted for 46.5 percent, 
29.9 percent, and 8.6 percent of the total net 
assets, respectively and remained the main 
components of the banks’ balance sheet.

Customer deposits, which are the main source 
of funding for the banks increased by Ksh.638.4 
billion (11.6 percent) from Ksh.5,480.8 billion 
in December 2024 to Ksh.6,119.2 billion in 
December 2025. 
In 2025, the banking sector capital and reserves 
increased by Ksh.220.3 billion (18.6 percent) 
from Ksh.1,183.6 billion in December 2024 
to Ksh.1,403.8 billion in December 2025. The 
increase in capital and reserves is attributable 
to increase in revaluation reserves and retained 
earnings. Revaluation reserves increased by 
Ksh.89.9 billion (1,831.5 percent) from negative 
Ksh.4.9 billion in December 2024 to Ksh.85.0 
billion in December 2025. Retained earnings 
increased by Ksh.89.4 billion (12.7 percent) from 
Ksh.706.6 billion in December 2024 to 796.1 
billion in December 2025.
3.12 Sectoral Distribution of Gross loans, 
Loan Accounts and Gross NonPerforming Loans
The largest proportion of the banking industry’s 
gross loans and advances were channeled to the 
Personal and Household, Trade, Manufacturing 
and Real Estate Sectors. In total, these four 
economic sectors accounted for 72.1 percent of 
gross loans in December 2025, as indicated in 
Table 18. Personal and Household, Trade, and 
Agriculture sectors accounted for the highest 
number of loan accounts with a total of 98.9 
percent. Trade, Real Estate Manufacturing, and 
Personal and Household sectors accounted for 
the highest value of non-performing loans by 
registering 72.6 percent. This was mainly due 
to a challenging business environment.

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 53
MACROECONOMIC CONDITIONS AND BANKING SECTOR PERFORMANCE
The concentration of non-performing loans was mainly in Trade, Real Estate, Manufacturing and 
Personal and Household sectors in December 2025. CBK will closely monitor the four economic 
sectors to ensure that commercial banks make adequate provisions for the loans in the four 
economic sectors to mitigate the risk of default.

3.12.1 Risk Classif ication of Assets, 
Provisioning and Limitation on Interest 
Recoverable on Non-Performing Loans
CBK’s Prudential Guideline on Risk 
Classification of Assets, Provisioning and 
Limitation on Interest Recoverable on NonPerforming Loans (CBK/ PG/ 04), requires 
commercial banks to classify loans and 
advances extended to their customers based 
on performance. The performance criteria 
is based on the repayment capability of the 
borrowers. The loans are classified as either 
normal, watch, substandard, doubtful or loss.
•	 Normal: Loans performing in accordance 
with the contractual terms and are up to 
date on repayments and are expected to 
continue in this condition.
•	 Watch: Loans which are generally past 
due by between 30 days and 90 days.
•	 Substandard: Loans which are generally 
past due for more than 90 days but less 
than 180 days.
•	 Doubtful: Loans which are generally past 
due for more than 180 days but less than 
360 days.
•	 Loss: Loans which are generally past due 
for 360 days or more.

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 54
MACROECONOMIC CONDITIONS AND BANKING SECTOR PERFORMANCE
The loans and advances in the Normal and 
Loss category increased by 10.2 percent and 
22.8 percent respectively. The loans and 
advances in the Watch, Substandard and 
Doubtful categories decreased by 7.4 percent, 
9.3 percent and 6.4 percent respectively 
(Table 19). The Normal category accounted 
for 74.7 percent of the total loans in 2025, 
compared to 72.4 percent in 2024 whereas, 
the Watch category accounted for 9.1 percent 
of the total loans in 2025, compared to 10.4 
percent in 2024. The Substandard, Doubtful 
and Loss categories accounted for 2.6 percent, 
8.3 percent, and 5.3 percent of the loan book 
in 2025, compared to 3.0 percent, 9.4 percent, 
and 4.6 percent in 2024.
The increase in the non-performing loans in 
the Loss category was mainly occasioned by 
challenges in the business environment.
The amount of loans in Normal and Loss 
categories increased in 2025, while those in 
Watch, Substandard and Doubtful categories 
decreased as shown in Table 19 and Chart 13.

3.13 Asset Quality
The stock of non-performing loans (NPLs) decreased by 0.1 percent to Ksh.696.9 billion in December 
2025 from Ksh.697.3 billion in December 2024. Asset quality, which is measured by the ratio of 
gross NPLs to gross loans improved with gross NPLs to gross loans ratio decreasing to 16.0 percent 
in December 2025 from 17.1 percent in December 2024 as shown in Table 20 and Appendix III. 
This was mainly due to a decrease in gross non-performing loans (1.1 percent) as compared to the 
increase in gross loans (6.8 percent). The improvement in asset quality was mainly due to new 
advances and repayments.

3.14 Capital Adequacy
The CBK Prudential Guideline on Capital 
Adequacy (CBK/PG/03) requires banks to 
adhere to the prescribed capital adequacy 
ratios. The current minimum regulatory 
capital adequacy ratios for Core Capital to 
Total Deposits, Core Capital and Total Capital 
to Total Risk Weighted Assets are 8.0 percent, 
10.5 percent, and 14.5 percent respectively. 
Core Capital to Total Risk Weighted Assets ratio 
increased from 17.3 percent in December 2024 
to 18.2 percent in December 2025. The Total 
Capital to Total Risk Weighted Assets ratio 
increased from 19.6 percent in December 2024 
to 20.7 percent in December 2025. The core 
capital to total deposits ratio also increased 
slightly from 18.0 percent in December 2024 
to 18.1 percent in December 2025. The Kenyan 
banking industry was therefore fully compliant 
with the capital adequacy ratios in 2025. Table 
21 presents the Kenyan banking sector capital 
adequacy trend from 2019 to 2025.

3.15 Liquidity
Liquidity held by commercial banks depicts 
their ability to fund increases in assets and 
meet obligations as they fall due. Liquidity 
is one of the important financial stability 
indicators. Liquidity shortfall in one bank can 
cause systemic crisis in the banking sector due 
to their interconnected operations.
The average liquidity ratio as at December 
2025, stood at 59.3 percent compared to 
55.8 percent registered in December 2024. 
The increase in the liquidity ratio is mainly 
attributed to a higher growth in total liquid 
assets as compared to the growth in shortterm liabilities. Total liquid assets grew by 
16.5 percent while the short-term liabilities 
increased by 9.6 percent. The banking sector’s 
average liquidity in 2025 was way above the 
statutory minimum requirement of 20 percent.
3.16 Profit and Loss
The banking sector registered an increase 
in profitability in 2025 with profit before tax 
increasing by Ksh.46.0 billion (17.7 percent) 
from Ksh.260.3 billion in December 2024 to 
Ksh.306.3 billion in December 2025 as shown 
in Table 22. The increase in prof itability 
was attributed to a higher decrease in total 
expenses (Ksh.67.4 billion) compared to a 
decrease in total income (Ksh.21.5 billion).

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA

3.16.1 Income
Total income for the banking sector decreased by 2.0 percent to Ksh.1,032.9 billion in December 
2025 from Ksh.1,054.4 billion in December 2024 as shown in Table 22. The decrease in income was 
largely attributed to a decrease in interest on advances (Ksh.40.6 billion), interest on placements 
(Ksh.3.3 billion) and other fees and commission income (Ksh.1.9 billion) between December 2024 
and December 2025.

3.16.2 Expenses
Banking sector total expenses decreased by Ksh.67.4 (8.5 percent) from Ksh.794.1 billion in December 
2024 to Ksh.726.7 billion in December 2025 (Table 22). The decrease was largely attributed to 
decreased interest expenses of Ksh.96.9 billion (25.1 percent) in 2025. Interest expenses accounted 
for 39.8 percent of the total banking sector expenses in 2025. Interest expense as a ratio of income 
decreased to 28.0 percent in 2025 from 36.6 percent in 2024.

Other expenses including training, advertising,

December 2025. Salaries and wages increased 
by Ksh.14.5 billion (9.9 percent) to Ksh.161.6 
billion in December 2025 from Ksh.147.1 billion 
in December 2024. Salaries and wages as a 
ratio of income increased from 18.5 percent 
in 2024 to 22.2 percent in 2025 reflecting a 
higher increase in staffing costs compared to

3.17 Performance Rating
The Central Bank of Kenya (CBK) uses the 
Capital Adequacy, Asset Quality, Management 
Quality, Earnings strength, and Liquidity 
position (CAMEL) rating system in assessing 
the soundness of the commercial banks. 
Commercial banks are ranked in a 5-scale 
rating as shown in Table 23.
The banking sector was on overall rated 
satisfactory in 2025 as was in 2024. The 
institutions rated strong, satisfactory, fair, 
marginal and unsatisfactory in December 2025 
were 10, 18, 8, 2 and 1 respectively, as compared 
to 10, 18, 8, 1 and 1 in December 2024, respectively 
(Table 23). The number of institutions rated 
satisfactory was 18 in both December 2024 and 
December 2025. The number of institutions 
rated fair was 8 in December 2024 same as in 
December 2025. However, the market share 
for banks rated satisfactory decreased to 49.7 
percent in December 2025, from 54.2 percent 
in December 2024.

3.18 Compliance with Supervisory and Regulatory Requirements
Thirty-five commercial banks were in violation of the Banking Act and CBK Prudential Guidelines 
as at December 31, 2025, compared to eleven commercial banks as at December 31, 2024. Most of 
the violations were with respect to non-compliance with the the Risk Based Credit Pricing Models 
(RBCPM), breach of single obligor limit and violation of the minimum absolute capital requirements 
of Ksh.3 billion as at December 2025.

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 59
The specific incidents of non-compliance 
noted as at December 31, 2025, were as follows:
i) Risk-Based Credit Pricing Model
CBK conducted target inspections in 
2025 on implementation of the Risk 
Based Credit Pricing Models (RBCPM) 
rolled out in 2019, by all commercial banks. 
Following the inspections, penalties were 
levied on 33 banks, and administrative 
actions were taken on 2 banks. 3 banks 
were fully compliant with the RBCPM.
ii) Single Obligor Limit
Ten commercial banks were in violation 
of Section 10 (1) of the Banking Act due 
to breach of the single obligor limit of 25 
percent of core capital. 
iii) Insider Lending
•	 Two banks were in violation of Section 
11(1) (f) of the Banking Act due to 
breach of the single insider borrower 
limit of 20 percent of the core capital.
•	 One bank was in violation of Section 
11(1) (g) of the Banking Act due to 
breach of the total insider borrower 
limit of 100 percent of the core capital.
iv) Prohibited Business
•	 Two commercial banks were in 
violation of Section 12 (c) of the Banking 
Act and CBK Prudential Guideline 
on Prohibited Business, CBK/PG/07, 
Clause 3.4.1 (c) for investing more than 
20 percent of the core capital in land 
and buildings.  
•	 Three commercial banks were in 
violation of CBK Prudential Guideline 
on Prohibited Business, CBK/PG/07, 
Clause 3.3, which restricts aggregate 
credit facilities to all large exposures 
to not more than 5 times of the 
institution’s core capital.  
v) Capital Adequacy Requirements
•	 Seven commercial banks were in 
violation of Section 7(1) of the Banking 
Act due to failure to maintain the 
minimum core capital required of 
Ksh.3 billion. 
•	 Five commercial banks were in 
violation of Section 18 of the Banking 
Act and CBK Prudential Guideline on 
Capital Adequacy, CBK/PG/03, Clause 
4.1.2 due to failure to meet the total 
capital to total risk weighted assets 
ratio of 14.5 percent. Four commercial 
banks did not meet the core capital 
to total risk weighted assets ratio of 
10.5 percent. While three commercial 
banks failed to meet the core capital 
to total deposit ratio of 8 percent. 
vi) Foreign Exchange Exposure
Two commercial banks were in in violation 
of Central Bank of Kenya Prudential 
Guideline on Foreign Exchange Exposure 
(CBK/PG/06) that requires institutions to 
maintain foreign exchange exposure at 
not more than 10 percent of core capital.
vii) Liquidity Management
One commercial bank was in violation 
of Section 19 (1) of the Banking Act and 
did not maintain the minimum statutory 
liquidity ratio of 20 percent.
viii) Corporate Governance
•	 Three banks were in violation of 
Section 13 (1) of the Banking Act and 
CBK/PG/07, Clause 3.5 which restricts 
ownership of an institution to any one 
person to a maximum of 25 percent.
•	 Three commercial banks were in 
violation of Clause 3.4.6 of CBK/PG/02 
on Board composition, which requires 
that an institution should have at 
least five directors and at least three 
fifths of the Directors should be Nonexecutive Directors.
MACROECONOMIC CONDITIONS AND BANKING SECTOR PERFORMANCE

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 60
•	 One institution was in violation of 
Section 13(4) of the Banking Act which 
requires that transfer of 5 percent 
shareholding of an institution must 
be approved by CBK.
Appropriate remedial actions were taken 
on the institutions concerned by the CBK in 
respect of the violations.
3.19 Performance of Microfinance Banks
The microfinance banking sector reported a 
cumulative pre-tax loss of Ksh.2.9 billion for the 
year ended December 31, 2025. As indicated 
in Table 24, the loss declined by 17.4 percent 
from Ksh.3.5 billion reported in the year ended 
December 31, 2024. Six microfinance banks 
(MFBs) reported profits, up from four MFBs in 
2024, while the remaining eight MFBs reported 
losses. The leading contributors to the sector 
losses in 2025, were Kenya Women MFB, Faulu 
MFB and SMEP MFB, which reported losses 
of Ksh.2.2 billion, Ksh.387 million and Ksh.270 
million, respectively.
The improvement in the performance of the 
sector was driven by decline in expenses by 
10.6 percent from Ksh.14.8 billion in 2024, to 
Ksh.12.5 billion in 2025. The decline in expenses 
is attributed to cost rationalisation measures 
implemented by the microfinance banks in 
2025, coupled with easing interest rates. The 
expenses that declined significantly include 
staff costs, interest and fees on borrowings, and 
interest and fees on deposits, which reduced 
by Ksh.841 million (19.1 percent), Ksh.519 million 
(42.5 percent) and Ksh.223 million (5.9 percent), 
respectively.
The return on assets improved by 0.8 percent 
from negative 6.1 percent in 2024 to negative 
5.3 percent in 2025, reflecting the reduction in 
losses by 17.4 percent relative to total assets. 
On the other hand, the return on shareholders’ 
funds deteriorated by 90.1 percent f rom 
negative 78.2 percent in 2024, to negative 
168.2 percent in 2025, due to the erosion of 
shareholders’ funds by the incurred losses of 
Ksh.2.9 billion.

In 2025, the ratios of core and total capital to total

were non-compliant with the capital adequacy

The sector’s liquidity ratio stood at 44.4 percent

minimum of 20 percent. Two MFBs failed to 
meet this requirement.
Operationally, the branch network expanded 
by two branches from 106 in 2024, to 108 in

opened one branch. On the other hand, the

3.20 Agency Banking
The delivery of financial services through the 
agent banking model continued to increase 
in 2025. During the year, 15 commercial banks 
and 4 microfinance banks (MFBs) contracted 
93,341 and 432 bank agents, respectively. As at 
December 2024, commercial banks recorded 
89,167 bank agents, while MFBs agents were 
539. The change was an increase of 4,174 
commercial bank agents (4.7 percent) and a 
decrease of 107 MFB agents (19.9 percent). 
Over 85 percent of the approved bank agents 
were concentrated in 3 banks with the largest 
physical branch presence namely, Equity Bank 
with 39,094 agents, KCB Bank Kenya Limited 
with 22,413 agents and Cooperative Bank of 
Kenya Limited with 17,914 agents. On the other 
hand, for the MFBs, over 95 percent of the 
agents were contracted by the three largest 
MFBs – Faulu Microfinance Bank Limited (133 
agents), Caritas Microfinance Bank Limited 
(159 agents), and Kenya Women Microfinance 
Bank Limited (127 agents).
(a) Number of Transactions 
The number of banking transactions 
undertaken through bank agents decreased 
by 20.2 percent from approximately 142.3 
million transactions recorded in 2024, to 113.5 
million transactions in December 2025. A brief 
summary is provided in Table 25 (a) below:

3.21 Credit Reference Bureaus 
Credit reports requests increased by 23 
percent from 38.6 million requests in 2024 
to 47.3 million requests in 2025. The growth 
in credit reports requests is attributable to 
growth in commercial banks’ lending to the 
private sector in 2025. Lending to private sector 
grew by 5.9 percent in December 2025 having 
improved from negative 2.9 percent in January 
2025.
The number of third-party data sources stood 
at 2,241 in the year 2025 having decreased from 
2,824 in the previous year. Credit Reference 
Bureaus (CRBs) terminated partnerships with 
621 non-deposit taking Saccos, accounting for 
30 percent from 2,040 in 2024 to 1,419 in 2025. 
The distribution of the third-party data sources 
in 2025 is shown in Table 26 (a).

3.22 Money Remittance Providers
Money Remittance Providers (MRPs) are licensed and regulated under the Money Remittance 
Regulations, 2013. The MRPs enable Kenyans living abroad to send money home safely and 
efficiently.
In 2025, remittance inflows reported by MRP’s amounted to Ksh.503.3 billion and contributed 
significantly to the country’s growth and development through consistent supply of foreign 
exchange and support to households. Remittance inflows increased by fourteen (14) percent from 
Ksh.440 billion in 2024 to Ksh.503 billion in 2025, as shown in table 27. The increase particularly 
noted in October to December 2025 is attributed to higher flows from the diaspora domiciled in 
the North America (United States of America and Canada) and Europe corridors.

Outflows amounted to Ksh.66.5 billion in 2025 and declined significantly from Ksh.91.8 billion in 
2024, as shown in Table 27. The reduction in remittance outflows is largely attributed to closure 
of several Non-Governmental Organizations (NGOs), including the United States Agency for 
International Development (USAID). The NGOs provided relief services within the African region, 
thereby supporting substantial financial inflows and outflows within the region. The shutdown of 
these organizations significantly affected countries such as Somalia, where NGO-related funding 
constitutes a substantial share of external financial inflows.

3.23 Mortgage Refinance Companies
A Mortgage Refinance Company (MRC) is 
established with the principal objective of 
provision of long-term finance to primary 
mortgage lenders (commercial banks, 
mortgage finance companies, microfinance 
banks and Savings and Credit Co-operatives) 
to increase the availability and affordability of 
mortgage loans to the public. The objectives 
of MRCs include:-
•	 Addressing maturity mismatch – Primary 
mortgage lenders (PMLs) often rely on 
short-term deposits to finance long-term 
mortgage loans. This creates a mismatch 
where liabilities (short-term deposits) 
mature much earlier than assets (longterm mortgage loans). MRCs therefore 
provide long-term funding to align the 
maturities of both. 
•	 Enhancing mortgage affordability – By 
offering long-term funding, MRCs enable 
lenders to extend mortgage repayment 
periods, reducing monthly instalments 
ultimately making home loans more 
affordable for borrowers.
•	 Liquidity for Lenders – Many banks and 
mortgage lenders rely on short-term 
deposits to fund long-term mortgage 
loans, creating a mismatch. MRC provides 
long-term funding, allowing lenders to 
offer more mortgages.
•	 Lower Interest Rates – By accessing 
stable and affordable long-term financing, 
mortgage lenders can pass on lower 
interest rates to borrowers, making home 
loans more affordable.
•	 Deepening the Capital Markets – MRCs 
raise funds by issuing bonds or other 
long-term securities. This not only provides 
them with sustainable capital but also 
helps develop local capital markets by 
offering investors long-term investment 
opportunities.
As part of its long-term development 
agenda, the Kenya Government identified 
the provision of Affordable Housing as one 
of its main development priorities alongside 
manufacturing, Universal Health Care and food 
security. The Government seeks to realize the 
housing goal through, among other strategies, 
addressing challenges in the domestic 
mortgage market. It was in this regard, that 
the National Treasury initiated amendments 
to the Central Bank of Kenya (CBK) Act, 
through the Finance Act, 2018. The CBK Act 
was amended to provide for licensing and 
regulation of the mortgage refinance business 
in Kenya. To operationalize the amendment, 
CBK developed the CBK (Mortgage Refinance 
Companies) Regulations, 2019, which were 
published in the Kenya Gazette in August 2019 
as Legal Notice No. 134 of 2019. 
Subsequently, CBK licensed the Kenya 
Mortgage Refinance Company (KMRC) as the 
first MRC on September 18, 2020. KMRC is a 
non-deposit taking financial institution whose 
mandate is to provide long-term funds to 
Primary Mortgage Lenders (PMLs) to increase 
the availability of affordable home loans to 
Kenyans. KMRC provides concessional, fixed, 
long-term finance to the PMLs, who then 
transfer the same benefits to their customers. 
This contributes towards making affordable 
home loans more accessible to especially, 
the moderate to low-income earners in the 
country.
•	 Shareholders: KMRC currently has 23 
shareholders. The National Treasury 
(25.3 percent), 2 Development Finance 
Institutions (DFIs) (22.9 percent), 8 
commercial banks (43.8 percent), one 
microfinance bank (0.6 percent), and 11 
SACCOs (7.5 percent). 
•	 Objective: KMRC principal objective is 
to provide cheaper long-term finance to 
primary mortgage lenders (commercial 
banks, mortgage f inance companies, 
MACROECONOMIC CONDITIONS AND BANKING SECTOR PERFORMANCE

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 67
microf inance banks and Savings and 
Credit Co-operatives). By doing so, KMRC 
enhances the availability and affordability of 
mortgages for the public while addressing 
the maturity mismatch between shortterm deposits and long-term mortgage 
loans.
•	 Regulation: KMRC is regulated by CBK as 
non-deposit taking financial institution, 
under the CBK (Mortgage Ref inance 
Companies) Regulations, 2019. The Capital 
Markets Authority (CMA) providing 
oversight over its bond issuance operations.
•	 Funding of KMRC: KMRC is funded 
through a combination of equity from 
shareholders, debt from development 
partners such as the World Bank and the 
African Development Bank (AfDB), and 
bond issuance. 
In 2025, the significant developments in 
respect to KMRC include:-
•	 KMRC’s total interest income in the year 
2025, reduced marginally by 0.7 percent 
from Ksh.3.21 billion in December 2024, 
to Ksh.3.19 billion in December 2025. This 
was attributed to the lower interest rate 
environment that prevailed in 2025.
•	 Profit before tax decreased by Ksh.453.1 
million (24.9 percent) from Ksh.1.82 billion 
in December 2024, to Ksh.1.36 billion in 
December 2025. mainly due to lower 
investment income arising from reduced 
rates of return (interest rates) during the 
year ended December 2025.
•	 KMRC continues to push for the availability 
and affordability of home loans to Kenyans 
by providing low interest, fixed rate, and 
long-term f inancing to participating 
banks and Saccos. The PMLs then pass 
this benefit to their clients through the 
provision of f ixed-rate home loans at 
single-digit interest rates. As of the end 
of December 2025, KMRC’s net loans and 
advances to PMLs increased by 65 percent 
from 11.9 billion in 2024, to Ksh.19.6 billion. 
•	 The loan repayment tenure has been 
lengthened from 11.71 years in 2024 to 
12.3 years in 2025, indicating improved 
affordability for borrowers. Notably, 48 
percent of KMRC-refinanced mortgages 
were issued to women, reflecting significant 
progress in advancing gender inclusion.
•	 Interest income f rom investments 
decreased by 15.7 percent from 2.72 billion 
in December 2024, to Ksh.2.30 billion in 
December 2025. This was attributed to the 
low interest environment that prevailed in 
2025.
•	 KMRC registered a 33.6 percent growth in 
total assets in the year 2025. The total assets 
as at December 2025, stood at Ksh.43.2 
billion, in comparison to Ksh.32.3 billion 
reported in the year ended 2024.
3.24 Digital Credit Providers
CBK has been at the forefront of advancing 
digital financial services. Through enabling 
regulation, the Bank has nurtured the growth 
of among others, digital lending. To this end, 
the year 2022 saw the implementation of the 
Central Bank of Kenya (CBK) Amendment Act, 
2021. The Act and the Digital Credit Providers 
Regulations expanded CBK’s regulatory and 
supervisory mandate by giving CBK the powers 
to license, regulate and supervise digital credit 
providers (DCPs) conducting digital credit 
business in Kenya. 
CBK has focused more on consumer protection, 
data governance, and market stability, 
with tighter scrutiny of DCPs. This includes 
enhanced requirements on transparency in 
pricing, responsible lending practices, and 
ethical use of customer data, alongside closer 
monitoring of compliance. As at December 
31, 2025, CBK had licensed 195 DCPs. The 
DCPs predominantly carry out their lending 
MACROECONOMIC CONDITIONS AND BANKING SECTOR PERFORMANCE

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 68
activities digitally including via Unstructured 
Supplementary Service Data (USSD) codes. 
Loan products range from education loans, 
development loans, short term personal loans, 
agricultural loans, asset financing, business 
loans, emergency loans, and motor vehicle 
loans. 
With the continued shift in customer 
preferences toward more convenient, 
technology-driven delivery channels, coupled 
with the rise in the number of licensed 
DCPs, the banking sector has experienced 
significant growth in digital lending between 
2023 and 2025. Under the oversight of CBK, 
this expansion has been accompanied by 
improved regulatory compliance and greater 
market discipline. Some highlights of the 
performance over the period (2023-2025) are 
indicated below: -
Lending by DCPs continued to grow rapidly 
in 2025, driven by an increase in the number 
of licensed providers, as illustrated in Chart 15
below. Gross outstanding loans nearly doubled, 
rising by 99.6 percent, from Ksh.55.2 billion in 
December 2024 to Ksh.110.1 billion in December 
2025. Over the same period, the number of 
licensed DCPs grew from 85 to 195.

4.1 Introduction
In the year 2025, there were no amendments 
to the Banking Act. CBK continued to enhance 
the regulatory framework to ensure that the 
sector remained stable and resilient.
4.2 The Banking (Penalties) Regulations, 
2025
The Banking (Penalties) Regulations, 2025, 
were made pursuant to section 55 of the 
Banking Act to strengthen the compliance 
culture in institutions licensed under the Act. 
The Regulations are intended to promote 
integrity within the banking sector, foster 
discipline in the conduct of banking business, 
and encourage compliance with the Banking 
Act and the Prudential Guidelines. They also 
establish an elaborate sanctions framework 
that is effective, proportionate, and dissuasive. 
This is aligned with international best practices 
and the requirements of global standardsetting bodies, including the Basel Committee 
on Banking Supervision (BCBS) and the 
Financial Action Task Force (FATF).
The Regulations differ in several significant 
respects from the repealed Banking (Penalties) 
Regulations, 1999. In particular, they:
•	 Clearly def ine the persons liable for 
penalties under the Banking Act for noncompliance with the Act and Prudential 
Guidelines. 
•	 Prescribe the violations under the Banking 
Act, the maximum penalty applicable to 
each violation, and the procedures for 
assessing and imposing such penalties. 
•	 Establish a comprehensive framework 
for the review of penalty decisions, in 
line with the constitutional right to fair 
administrative action under Article 47 of 
the Constitution and section 4 of the Fair 
Administrative Action Act. 
•	 Specify the procedures for the payment 
of monetary penalties imposed on an 
institution or any person responsible for 
contraventions of the Banking Act, as well 
as any guidelines or directions issued by 
the Central Bank of Kenya (CBK). 
•	 Incorporate the enhanced penalty 
regime introduced by the Business Laws 
(Amendment) Act, 2024, which empowers 
the CBK, under section 55(3) of the Banking 
Act, to impose a penalty of up to three 
times the gross amount of any monetary 
gain realized or loss avoided as a result 
of an institution’s non-compliance. This 
strengthens the deterrent effect of the 
enforcement framework. 
The Regulations were approved by the National 
Assembly Committee on Delegated Legislation 
on December 2, 2025. 
4.3 Host Country Assessments
Several Kenyan supervised banking groups 
have establishments in seven (7) jurisdictions, 
six (6) in EAC partner states and one (1) non EAC 
country. It is critical for CBK to understand the 
legal, supervisory and resolution frameworks 
in these jurisdictions where Kenyan banking 
groups have establishments since risks 
emanating from the jurisdictions can have 
profound effects on the banking groups.
In March 2015, CBK in partnership with IMF’s 
AFRITAC East developed a structured approach 
for the assessment of the quality of supervision 
undertaken by host countries where Kenyan 
banking groups have establishments. This 
was part of the implementation of Risk Based 
Supervision Framework on Consolidated 
Supervision. Assessment on the quality of 
host country supervision is required under 
Basel Core Principle (BCP) 12, on consolidated 
supervision. 
The home -host assessments enable CBK to 
form its own supervisory view on the host 
supervisor. Depending on the findings of the 
DEVELOPMENTS IN THE SUPERVISORY FRAMEWORK

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025
72
assessment, CBK may make adjustment in its 
supervisory processes for hosted groups.
Since 2015, when the assessment criterion was 
developed, BSD has conducted assessments 
in all the seven (7) jurisdictions where Kenyan 
banks have establishments. These previous 
assessments revealed that, amongst the 
seven jurisdictions, the supervisory practices, 
legal and regulatory frameworks in four (4) 
jurisdictions were largely adequate and could 
be relied upon by CBK in the supervision 
of the hosted subsidiaries. Although gaps 
were identified in the legal and supervisory 
frameworks of two (2) jurisdictions, it was 
observed that considerable efforts were 
being undertaken by the host countries to 
improve the quality of their frameworks. 
Further, the assessments established that in 
one (1) jurisdiction, the legal and supervisory 
framework was inadequate.
In 2025, CBK undertook reassessment of 
the two jurisdictions where gaps had been 
identif ied in their legal and supervisory 
frameworks during the previous assessments. 
The reassessments noted that there had not 
been significant improvement in both the 
legal, regulatory and supervisory practices 
since the last reviews. 
For one of the jurisdictions, there were still 
outstanding significant gaps in the legal 
framework. The outstanding gaps could inhibit 
the effectiveness in supervision of hosted 
subsidiaries and branches of Kenyan banking 
groups. Further, there were still weaknesses 
in the supervisory practice. For example, the 
supervisor did not have a documented risk 
management framework for the banking 
sector. Bank resolution mechanisms have also 
not been fully implemented.
In the second jurisdiction, there were 
considerable improvements in the legal 
framework since the last review. However, 
there were gaps in the supervisory practices 
including failure to implement risk-based 
supervision and staff capacity challenges. 
On bank resolutions, the jurisdiction was 
in nascent stages of developing a deposit 
protection scheme. 
CBK will continue to monitor developments 
in legal framework, supervisory practices 
and deposit protection mechanisms in the 
two jurisdictions and make adjustments in 
the supervisory approaches for the banking 
groups in the hosted subsidiaries in light of 
the identified weaknesses.
4.4 Greening of the Financial System
In April 2025, CBK issued the Climate Risk 
Disclosure Framework (CRDF) for the Banking 
Sector and the Kenya Green Finance Taxonomy 
(KGFT). Both frameworks were developed under 
a Technical Assistance Programme provided 
to CBK by the European Investment Bank 
(EIB), following the signing of a Cooperation 
Agreement in October 2023.
Both the CRDF and the KGFT development 
processes began in October 2023 with the 
formation of technical working groups, 
followed by initial stakeholder consultations, 
collection of climate data f rom relevant 
Government Ministries and State Departments, 
and issuance of draft frameworks for further 
consultation. The f inal f rameworks were 
published in April 2025.
In addition, the KGFT development process 
included benchmarking against international 
best practices, identification of users and 
beneficiaries, and prioritization of sectors to 
ensure alignment with global standards while 
reflecting local circumstances. The draft KGFT 
was piloted among selected commercial banks 
in October 2024 to test its usability.
DEVELOPMENTS IN THE SUPERVISORY FRAMEWORK

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 73
Capacity-building workshops on both 
frameworks were delivered to commercial 
banks and CBK staff throughout the 
programme period.
4.4.1 Climate Risk Disclosure Framework 
for the Banking Sector
The CRDF signifies a commitment to promoting 
transparency, accountability, and sustainability 
in the financial sector. It is aligned with the 
best global practices and standards, such 
as the International Financial Reporting 
Standards (IFRS) S2. It also aligns with the 
Basel Committee on Banking Supervision 
principles  for effective management and 
supervision of climate-related financial risks. 
The framework is anchored on four key pillars: 
Governance, Strategy, Risk Management, and 
Metrics and Targets.
Reporting Period for the CRDF:
i) Voluntary reporting for the large peer 
group banks will apply to accounting 
periods beginning on or after January 1, 
2025.
ii) Voluntary reporting for medium and small 
peer groups will apply to accounting 
periods beginning on or after January 1, 
2026.
iii) Mandatory reporting for all commercial 
banks will apply to accounting periods 
beginning on or after January 1, 2027.
4.4.2 Kenya Green Finance Taxonomy
The KGFT is an official classification framework 
that def ines the minimum set of assets, 
projects, activities, and sectors eligible to be 
recognized as “green” or environmentally 
sustainable. It provides a standardized reference 
to help investors, financial institutions, and 
service providers identify, track, verify, and 
disclose green activities, thereby enhancing 
transparency, credibility, and accountability in 
climate and environmental finance.
The KGFT has identified 11 priority economic 
sectors21 and activities that make a significant 
contribution to climate change mitigation and 
adaptation. These sectors have been prioritized 
particularly where climate-related impacts are 
most material within the local context. 
How to apply KGFT:
i) Assess Eligibility: Determine whether the 
economic activity is covered by the KGFT.
ii) Determine Alignment : Eva l u a te 
compliance with the Technical Screening 
Criteria22 as outlined below: 
•	 Makes Significant Contribution to at 
least one environmental objective.
•	 Does No Significant Harm to the other 
environmental objectives.
•	 Meets Minimum Social Safeguards 
and where applicable, applies remedial 
measures to support transition.
iii) Disclose Results: Report on the level of 
KGFT alignment at the asset, company, 
fund, or portfolio level.
KGFT Regulatory Application
In its current form, the KGFT first edition is 
voluntary tool without a formal framework for 
enforcement. However, the CBK aims to allow 
for a period of voluntary uptake of disclosure 
and alignment according to the KGFT within 
the banking sector, ahead of mandatory 
regulation. The manner, structure and timeline 
of this regulation is still under review.
21  The 11 priority economic sectors include: Manufacturing; 
Agriculture, Forestry and Fishing; Mining and Quarrying; 
Electricity, gas, steam and air conditioning; Water 
supply, Sewerage, Waste management and remediation; 
Transportation and Storage; Real Estate; Construction; 
Information and Communication.
22 Technical Screening Criteria establishes the minimum 
performance requirements that an economic activity must 
meet to be classified as environmentally sustainable to 
demonstrate an economic activity’s alignment with the 
taxonomy.
