CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025
74
4.5 Liquidity Standards and Leverage 
Ratios
In order to strengthen the banking sector 
liquidity and capital adequacy, CBK issued 
guidelines on Liquidity Coverage Ratio (LCR), 
Net Stable Funding Ratio (NSFR) and Leverage 
Ratio (LR) in April 2025. The LCR, NSFR and LR 
regulatory ratios, were introduced as part of 
Basel III regulatory reforms in response to the 
Global Financial Crisis.23 The introduction of 
these ratios was necessitated by the failure of 
many banks to adequately measure, manage 
and control their liquidity and capital adequacy 
risks. The Basel III framework uses LCR, NSFR, 
and LR to ensure banks maintain short-term 
liquidity, long-term funding stability, and limit 
excessive leverage.
CBK introduced LCR and NSFR in the Risk 
Management Guidelines of 2013, as best 
practices for familiarization and benchmarking 
in preparation for future mandating. The 
issuance in April 2025 of the guidelines on 
LCR, NSFR, and LR was an important step in 
strengthening the banking sector’s liquidity 
and capital adequacy and aligning the 
requirements with the Basel III framework. 
These guidelines are intended to enhance 
the sector’s liquidity and capital frameworks, 
promoting greater financial stability and 
resilience.
Prior to issuance of the guidelines on LCR, 
NSFR, and LR, banks were required to maintain 
a minimum statutory liquidity ratio of 20 
percent, measured by total liquid assets to total 
short-term liabilities (three months’ liabilities). 
The minimum statutory liquidity framework 
focuses on short-term liquidity resilience and 
does not provide banks with resilience to 
potential liquidity disruptions for long-term 
needs. Accordingly, LCR and NSFR strengthen 
liquidity resilience of the banking sector by 
23  Basel III is a set of measures to strengthen bank regulation, 
supervision and risk management after the 2007-09 crisis.
focusing on stability during long term periods 
of liquidity disruptions. Accordingly, the 
minimum liquidity ratio is now supplemented 
by LCR and NSFR.
Similarly, CBK introduced the Internal Capital 
Adequacy Assessment Process (ICAAP) for 
Capital Adequacy Framework. As a key tool in a 
bank’s risk management framework, ICAAP is 
a comprehensive, forward-looking, and flexible 
approach that enhances governance, capital 
efficiency, regulatory compliance, and longterm sustainability. However, its weaknesses 
include subjectivity, complexity, over-reliance 
on models, and challenges in adapting to 
emerging risks. This can lead to insufficient 
risk coverage and capital buffers, particularly 
in times of financial stress. Accordingly, the 
LR is a non-risk basked measure of a bank’s 
capital adequacy and is proposed to act as a 
supplementary measure to ICAAP, which is a 
risk-based capital requirements measure.
4.5.1 Liquidity Coverage Ratio (LCR)
The LCR Guideline aims to promote the 
banks’ short-term resilience by requiring 
maintenance of sufficient High-Quality Liquid 
Assets (HQLA) that enable banks to survive 
acute stress lasting 30 calendar days. HQLA 
consists of assets that can be converted into 
cash at little or no loss of value, to meet a 
bank’s liquidity needs for a 30-calendar day 
liquidity stress scenario. These assets include 
bank coins and notes, CBK reserves that can be 
drawn in times of stress, and securities issued 
or guaranteed by the Government of Kenya.
LCR is computed by dividing a bank’s stock of 
HQLA by its total net cash outflows expected 
over the next 30 calendar days under a stress 
scenario. The guideline requires that the value 
of the ratio be at least 100 percent. The LCR is 
reported to CBK on a monthly basis.
DEVELOPMENTS IN THE SUPERVISORY FRAMEWORK

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 75
4.5.2 Net Stable Funding Ratio (NSFR)
The NSFR Guideline aims to promote resilience 
over a longer-term horizon by incentivizing 
banks to fund activities with more stable 
sources of funding. Banks are to maintain 
a stable funding profile in relation to the 
composition of their assets and off-balance 
sheet activities. NSFR is computed by dividing 
the bank’s available stable funding by required 
stable funding and should be at least 100 
percent. The NSFR is reported semi-annually.
4.5.3 Leverage Ratio (LR)
The LR is designed to act as a supplementary 
measure to the risk-based capital requirements. 
It helps determine whether a bank has enough 
capital to handle financial risks. It also helps to 
reduce excessive borrowing and strengthen 
financial stability by providing a non-risk-based 
measure of a bank’s capital adequacy. The LR 
measures the bank’s core capital relative to its 
total exposure, without adjusting for risk. The 
guideline requires that banks meet a 4 percent 
leverage ratio at all times. LR is computed 
by dividing Tier 1 Capital by the bank’s total 
exposure measure (including on-balancesheet assets and certain off-balance-sheet 
exposures). The LR is reported on a quarterly 
basis.
4.6 Credit Information Sharing (CIS)
The Central Bank of Kenya (CBK) continued 
with the journey of ref ining the Credit 
Information Sharing Framework (CIS) to 
achieve the intended objective of managing 
credit risk. To address strategic and operational 
challenges hampering the operation of the 
CIS mechanism, CBK with technical assistance 
of World Bank Group’s International Finance 
Corporation launched the Kenya Credit 
Reporting Strengthening Project (KCRSP). 
The project, currently in Phase 2 running from 
July 2023 to June 2028, aims at achieving the 
following milestones:
a) Supporting the CIS mechanism through 
enhancing predictiveness of credit 
scores, building transparency, enhancing 
CIS resilience against future crises and 
expanding the CIS regulatory perimeter 
through on-boarding digital lenders. 
b) Implementing risk-based credit pricing 
to support implementation of the 
CBK Banking Sector Charter, 2019 by 
commercial banks and non-bank lenders. 
c) Supporting Micro, Small and Medium 
Enterprises (MSMEs) through promoting 
the use of alternative data in credit scoring 
to widen sources of credit information.
In August 2025, CBK granted approval for 
the roll out of an industry data validation and 
submission tool, christened CIS ValiData (a 
standardized platform to validate data quality 
by Credit Information Providers (CIPs) before 
simultaneous submission to all the three 
licensed bureaus). The validation is done 
in accordance with the Data Specification 
Template (DST Version 4)24 and Validation 
Rules. The tool is expected to provide the 
following benefits to the Credit Information 
Sharing (CIS) Framework:
•	 To provide an error log to the CIPs before 
submission of data to bureaus. The CIPs 
will have an opportunity to correct the 
data before submission leading to overall 
improvement in the quality of submitted 
data.
•	 To facilitate simultaneous submission of 
records to all licensed bureaus, easing the 
CIPs workload and ensuring similar data 
is submitted to all bureaus.
•	 CIS Kenya will have access to reports on 
the quality of data submitted by each CIP. 
This will inform targeted interventions 
by CIS Kenya on CIPs with data quality 
challenges.
24 A detailed data sharing framework to ensure uniformity of 
the data shared by CIPs to all three bureaus.

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 76
4.7 Granular Data Integration project
During the year, the Central Bank of Kenya 
(CBK) continued implementation of the 
Granular Data Integration (GDI) project – a 
strategic supervisory technology initiative 
designed to transform the Bank’s regulatory 
reporting framework. The project encompasses 
the development of a contemporary data lake 
environment underpinned by an Enterprise 
Data Warehouse (EDW), which is fully 
integrated with the systems of supervised 
institutions through secure Application 
Programming Interfaces (APIs).
The GDI project represents a fundamental 
shift in regulatory reporting, moving from 
aggregated institutional reporting to the 
collection of granular, primary-level data, 
such as individual account, customer, and 
transactional records. This transformation in 
the data collection methodology enables a 
richer, more precise understanding of the risk 
landscape within supervised institutions and 
across the financial system as a whole.
The GDI platform is designed to support 
the entire data lifecycle, from secure data 
collection and validation to structured 
storage, advanced supervisory analytics, and 
regulatory reporting. Once fully operational, 
it will significantly strengthen CBK’s off-site 
surveillance capabilities, enable more targeted 
and risk-based on-site supervision, and support 
near real-time monitoring of developments 
across the financial sector.
In addition to enhancing supervisory 
effectiveness, the platform is designed to 
deliver tangible operational benef its to 
supervised institutions, including:
•	 Improved reporting efficiency through 
automated, API-enabled data submission 
processes that streamline reporting 
workflows and reduce manual effort.
•	 Timely access to industry statistics and 
peer benchmarking insights, empowering 
institutions to strengthen strategic 
planning, performance monitoring, and 
risk management practices.
•	 Improved data quality through automated 
validation and feedback mechanisms that 
enable institutions to identify and address 
data issues at the point of submission.
•	 Stronger data governance by promoting 
greater accountability, consistency, and 
control over data management practices 
across supervised institutions.
•	 Alignment with international statistical 
standards, including the System of National 
Accounts (SNA) and the International 
Standard Industrial Classification of All 
Economic Activities (ISIC), enhancing data 
comparability, consistency, and regulatory 
compliance.
4.8 Developments in Anti-Money 
Laundering, Combating the 
Financing of Terrorism and 
Combating Proliferation Financing
4.8.1 Implementation of the FATF Action 
Plan for Kenya
Following the greylisting of Kenya by the 
Financial Action Task Force (FATF) in February 
2024, Kenya made a high-level political 
commitment to work with the FATF to address 
the action items set out under the FATF Action 
Plan for Kenya.
During 2025, CBK implemented a range of 
regulatory, supervisory and capacity-building 
initiatives aimed at addressing these action 
items and strengthening the effectiveness 
of the country’s anti-money laundering, 
countering the financing of terrorism and 
countering proliferation financing (AML/CFT/
CPF) framework. These initiatives are outlined 
below.

a) Immediate Outcome 3 (IO3) – Supervision 
During the reporting period, CBK implemented 
the following supervisory initiatives:
•	 Strengthening supervisory capacity:
CBK enhanced the staffing and financial

The Act:
o Establishes a legislative framework 
for the licensing and regulation of 
the activities of virtual asset service 
providers (VASPs) in and from Kenya.
o Designates the Capital Markets 
Authority (CMA) and the CBK as the 
regulatory authorities responsible, 
among other functions, for licensing 
virtual asset service providers as 
defined under the Act.
o Provides for supervisory powers of the 
regulators for AML/CFT/CPF purposes, 
including the authority to undertake 
inspections, investigations and to take 
enforcement action in cases of noncompliance with applicable AML/CFT/
CPF laws and regulations.

Financial Sanctions (TFS) and the Physical 
Cross-Border Movement of Cash.

and the Physical Cross-Border Movement

regarding the implementation of AML/
CFT/CPF preventive measures through 
scheduled bilateral regulatory meetings 
with financial 
institutions.
4.8.2 Implementation of the 
Recommendations of the Mutual 
Evaluation Report (MER) for Kenya
Under the ESAAMLG mutual evaluation 
framework, countries are assessed not only on 
technical compliance with AML/CFT standards 
but also on the effectiveness of their systems. 
This effectiveness is measured through a set 
of Immediate Outcomes (IOs), each focusing 
on a specific area, such as the supervision of 
financial institutions or the implementation 
of preventive measures. During the mutual 
evaluation process, Key Recommended Actions 
(KRAs) are identified for each Immediate 
Outcome, representing priority actions that a 
country needs to implement to address gaps 
in its AML/CFT framework. 
Post mutual evaluation, ESAAMLG member 
countries are required to report progress on 
these KRAs, providing evidence of legislative, 
regulatory, supervisory, and capacity-building 
measures taken. It should be noted that while 
most of these actions address the FATF Action 
DEVELOPMENTS IN THE SUPERVISORY FRAMEWORK

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 78
Plan under the greylisting process, some 
Immediate Outcome items reported may not 
form part of the formal FATF Action Plan but 
remain important for enhancing the overall 
effectiveness of Kenya’s AML/CFT framework.
Some of the KRAs implemented by CBK in 2025 
are as follows:
•	 Guidance on the ML/TF National Risk 
Assessment: CBK, in June 2025 circulated 
copies of both the ML and TF National

circular in June 2025, to all licensees, 
including commercial banks, microfinance banks, mobile money operators,

and digital credit providers for information 
and guidance.
•	 Capacity Building initiatives on the

4.9 Regulation of Virtual Asset Service 
Providers
Virtual Assets (VAs) have emerged as an 
area of interest for the financial sector. The 
Innovation Survey 2025 Report25 indicated 
that 35 percent of institutions identif ied 
virtual assets as important in innovation. 
Kenya’s financial sector regulators, that is, 
CBK, the Capital Markets Authority (CMA), 
the Sacco Societies Regulatory Authority 
(SASRA), the Retirement Benefits Authority 
(RBA), and the Insurance Regulatory Authority 
(IRA), along with the Ministry responsible 
for Cooperatives, and Financial Reporting 
Centre (FRC), have been taking proactive 
measures and closely monitoring the growing 
interest and adoption of VAs in the Country. 
The country has generally taken a cautious 
approach to VAs, with regulators emphasizing 
the need for consumer protection, Anti-Money 
Laundering, Counter Financing of Terrorism 
and Counter-Proliferation Financing (AML/
CFT/CPF) compliance, and the prevention of 
illicit activities.
The 2023 National Money Laundering and 
Terrorism Financing (ML/TF) risk assessment 
on VAs and Virtual Asset Service Providers 
(VASPs),26 identified and assessed the ML and 
TF threats and vulnerabilities associated with 
VAs and VASPs. 
The assessment found that there was 
continued adoption of VAs in the country. 
However, VAs have inherent vulnerabilities 
including rapid transaction speed, ability to 
transact pseudonymously/anonymously, and 
the cross-border nature of such transactions. 
Accordingly, the assessment noted VA and 
VASP activities could be exploited by criminals 
due to their inherent vulnerabilities and the 
lack of regulatory framework for VAs and 
25  https://www.centralbank.go.ke/2026/05/07/banking-sectorinnovation-survey-2025/ 
26  https://www.frc.go.ke/wp-content/uploads/2024/02/VAs-andVASPs-ML_TF-Risk-Assessment-Report-1.pdf
DEVELOPMENTS IN THE SUPERVISORY FRAMEWORK

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 79
VASPs. Consequently, it was recommended 
that Kenya regulates VA activities to address 
the identified risks and strengthen the overall 
AML/CFT framework.
In 2024, the National Treasury and Economic 
Planning, established a Multi-Agency Task 
Force to develop a Policy and Regulatory 
Framework for Virtual Assets and Virtual Asset 
Service Providers (VASPs). The Task Force is 
spearheaded by the National Treasury and 
draws membership from CBK, CMA, IRA, 
SASRA, RBA, FRC, State Law Office, State 
Department for ICT and the Digital Economy 
(SDICT&DE), Kenya Revenue Authority (KRA), 
Ministry of Interior, and the National Computer 
and Cybercrime Coordination Committee 
(NC4). The cross-governmental approach 
was adopted in order to comprehensively 
address the VA-related concerns of different 
stakeholders and to provide a coordinated 
approach towards developing an all-inclusive 
policy and regulatory framework.
The Task Force developed the draft National 
Policy on VAs and VASPs, as well as the Virtual 
Asset Service Providers Bill, 2025. Below are 
key highlights of the legal and regulatory 
framework for VAs and VASPs in Kenya.
4.9.1 National Policy on VAs and VASPs
The National Policy on VAs and VASPs was 
approved by Cabinet in March 2025. The Policy 
outlines the developments in VAs and VASPs 
in Kenya, highlighting key activities as per the 
National Risk Assessment on ML and TF Risks of 
VAs and VASPs. It also takes stock of regulatory 
developments globally and domestically, 
citing the various cautionary notices by CBK 
and other financial sector regulators against 
dealing with unregulated financial service 
providers. The main objective of the Policy is to 
guide the development of a fair, competitive, 
and stable market for VAs and VASPs in Kenya, 
with the following specific objectives:
i) Guide the development of a legal 
framework governing VAs and VASPs.
ii) Promote fair and efficient market for VAs 
and VASPs. 
iii) Ensure sound risk management for VAs 
and VASPs. 
iv) Promote financial literacy and innovation 
on VAs and VASPs.
4.9.2 Virtual Asset Service Providers Act (No. 
20 of 2025)
The Virtual Assets Service Providers Act, 2025 
(Act No. 20 of 2025) was assented to on October 
15, 2025. The Act was gazetted on October 21, 
2025, and came into effect on November 4, 
2025. 
The Act provides the legal mandate for 
regulation of VA activities and designates 
CBK and CMA as the relevant regulatory 
authorities to license and supervise VASPs. 
CBK is mandated to oversee the  following 
categories of VASPs: 
i) VA Wallet Providers.
ii) VA Payment Processors. 
iii) VA Offering Providers dealing with issuance 
of stablecoin. 
CMA is mandated to oversee the following 
categories of VASPs: 
i) VA Exchanges. 
ii) VA Brokers. 
iii) VA Investment Advisors. 
iv) VA Managers. 
v) VA Offering Providers dealing with Initial 
Coin Offerings, VA Tokenization, and VA 
Token Issuance Platforms.

BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 80
4.9.3 Draft Virtual Asset Service Providers 
Regulations
Pursuant to  section 47  of the Virtual Asset 
Service Providers Act, 2025 (Act No. 20 
of 2025), persons providing virtual asset 
services  are required to  comply with  the 
provisions of the Act within one year of 
commencement. Development of draft Virtual 
Asset Service Providers Regulations was 
ongoing as of December 2025 to provide for 
the procedures for licensing of VASPS, as well 
as implement other key aspects of the Act.

REGIONAL AND INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENTS AND INITIATIVES
5.1 Introduction
CBK is committed to fostering f inancial 
stability, regional integration, and sustainable 
finance in Kenya and the wider region. In 2025, 
CBK participated in a number of regional and 
global initiatives that fall within its supervisory 
mandate, either by virtue of its membership 
in regional/global forums or by invitation. 
Through these engagements, CBK stays 
updated on regional and global regulatory 
changes, keeps abreast of emerging issues, 
gains exposure to international developments, 
and offers opportunities to enhance the 
capacity of CBK staff.
In 2025, CBK hosted and participated in 
various meetings and training forums which 
were held both in person and virtually. In the 
regional and global arena, BSD participated 
in forums hosted by the Alliance for Financial 
Inclusion (AFI), International Monetary 
Fund (IMF)’s African Training Institute, the 
COMESA Competition Commission, COMESA 
Financial System Development and Stability 
Subcommittee, Association of African Central 
Banks (AACB), Financial Reporting Centre 
(FRC), Financial Sector Deepening Kenya 
(FSD-K), Islamic Financial Services Board 
(IFSB), European Investment Bank (EIB), and 
COMESA Monetary Institute. These forums 
discussed various issues affecting the financial 
sector locally, regionally and globally. The areas 
of engagement included capacity building, 
policy development, climate-related risk 
management, sustainable finance, digital 
finance, Supervisory Technologies, liquidity 
management innovation as well as financial 
inclusion.
BSD also participated in meetings convened 
under the auspices of the Monetary Affairs 
Committee (MAC). These meetings aimed 
to promote convergence of regulatory and 
supervisory frameworks in support of the 
East African Community’s transition towards 
the proposed East African Monetary Union. 
Further, Bank Supervision staff continued 
to enhance their skills and knowledge on 
regulatory and supervisory matters through 
the Financial Stability Institute’s (FSI) on-line 
learning tool, FSI Connect.
5.2 R e g i o n a l a n d I n te rn a t i o n a l 
Initiatives
5.2.1 Monetary Affairs Committee of the 
East African Community
The Monetary Affairs Committee (MAC) of the 
East African Community (EAC) is a committee 
within EAC that is responsible for overseeing 
the monetary policies and financial stability 
of the EAC member states. MAC is composed 
of the Governors of the Central Banks of the 
eight EAC Partner States27. The main task of 
MAC is to coordinate efforts by EAC Central 
Banks towards developing and implementing 
monetary policies that promote price stability, 
financial stability, development and economic 
growth in the region. MAC aims at facilitating 
the establishment of the envisaged East 
African Monetary Union (EAMU). 
MAC initiatives focus on areas pertinent to 
financial stability as well as emerging crosscutting issues. Some of the key initiatives 
include harmonization of monetary policies 
and supervisory rules and practices, the 
establishment of a regional payment 
and settlement system, f inancial sector 
development, capacity building, and regional 
integration. These initiatives are implemented 
through committees or Technical Working 
Groups (TWG) composed of technical officials 
from the member Partner States. The technical 
officials undertake various assignments and 
initiatives, which are then overseen by the 
Governors. 
27  Kenya, Uganda, Tanzania, Rwanda, Burundi, South Sudan, 
Democratic Republic of Congo, and Somalia.

their bids to the ESAAMLG Secretariat in 
September 2025.
•	 The 8th ESAAMLG Public-Private Sector 
Dialogue (PPSD), held on August 29-
30, 2025, in Addis Ababa, Ethiopia, 
provided a platform for regulators, 
supervisors, financial institutions and 
other stakeholders to discuss AML/CFT/
PF opportunities and challenges in 
the region’s capital markets under the 
theme "AML/CFT opportunities and 
challenges in Capital Markets within the 
ESAAMLG Region”. The Dialogue focused 
on beneficial ownership transparency, 
sanctions implementation, and riskbased supervision, while promoting the 
exchange of best practices to strengthen 
AML/CFT/CPF f rameworks across the 
ESAAMLG region.
5.2.4 East African Monetary Union (EAMU)
The EAMU Protocol, adopted under the EAC 
Treaty and signed on 30 November 2013, was 
ratified in January 2015, setting a ten-year 
roadmap toward the introduction of a single 
currency by 2024. However, due to delays in 
meeting convergence criteria and setting up 
necessary institutions, the EAC has extended 
this deadline to 2031. This extension allows 
member states additional time to harmonize 
monetary and fiscal policies, establish requisite 
institutions like the East African Monetary 
Institute, and ensure macroeconomic stability 
across the region.
CBK continues to participate in various 
meetings organized by MAC as part of ensuring 
the operationalization of the Protocol by 2031. 
As of end of 2025, the operationalization of 
EAMU was in progress.

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 85
REGIONAL AND INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENTS AND INITIATIVES
5.2.5 Alliance for Financial Inclusion (AFI)
AFI is a policy leadership alliance owned and 
led by member central banks and financial 
regulatory institutions. It was launched in 
September 2008, with the aim of making 
financial services more accessible to the 
world’s unbanked. The AFI network is currently 
composed of a voluntary membership and 
CBK being a member of AFI since its inception. 
Over its 17 years of existence, AFI has facilitated 
the implementation of impactful financial 
inclusion policy changes by providing a 
platform for peer learning, knowledge sharing 
and peer transformation across its network. 
This has been achieved through a cooperation 
model that is made up of;
a. Country-led approaches, which allows AFI 
members to support countries in identifying 
their unique needs and challenges and 
come up with customized high-impact 
solutions.
b. Peer-to-peer engagement, which
promotes a culture of equal opportunities 
for members by allowing all members to 
share their experiences as well as learn from 
each other.
AFI has seven working groups, which are the 
primary source of financial inclusion policy 
development and serve as communities of 
practice on key financial inclusion issues. 
The working groups provide a platform for 
knowledge exchange and peer learning, 
enabling policymakers to share, deliberate and 
deepen their knowledge and understanding 
of key financial inclusion issues. 
CBK continues to benefit from its membership 
in AFI through various resources support 
as well as participation in various forums as 
follows: -
•	 CBK hosted AFI’s Chief Executive Officer 
and his Africa Regional leadership team on 
February 28, 2025. The meeting discussed 
the ongoing and potential collaborations 
between AFI and CBK. 
•	 CBK nominated a speaker at the Virtual 
In-Country Training on Enabling Financial 
Inclusion for Persons with Disabilities 
held on April 16 and 17, 2025. The training 
was tailored for the technical staff of the 
Agency of the Republic of Kazakhstan for 
Regulation and Development of Financial 
Market and local stakeholders who are 
engaged in initiatives supporting PWDs. 
The main objectives of the training were 
to identify barriers faced by financial 
regulators in implementing policies 
that support PWDs in accessing formal 
financial services. Further, the training 
aimed at evaluating approaches for 
developing effective financial policies for 
PWDs.
•	 Participated in the 14th Annual Roundtable 
of the Leaders of Af rican Financial 
Inclusion Policy Initiative (AfPI) held in 
Accra, Ghana from June 17 - 19, 2025. 
AfPI availed a platform for leaders from 
AFI member institutions in Af rica to 
engage in discussions on enhancing 
the cyber resilience of Africa’s digital 
financial services. The dialogue focused 
on strengthening the role of financial 
regulators in policy formulation, risk 
management, regulatory compliance, and 
cross border cooperation to foster a secure, 
inclusive and sustainable digital financial 
ecosystem.
•	 Participated in the Reserve Bank of 
Malawi-AFI Joint Learning Program (JLP) 
on Empowering Women-Led MSMEs 
through financial education initiatives 
held in Malawi f rom July 1 – 4, 2025. 
During the JLP, AFI members explored 
the key requirements for successfully 
implementing strategic initiatives to

5.2.6 Financial Stability Board Regional 
Consultative Group for Sub-Saharan 
Africa
The Financial Stability Board (FSB), Regional 
Consultative Group for Sub-Saharan Africa 
was held in Livingstone, Zambia in June 2025, 
and attended by representatives from FSBmember institutions in Sub-Saharan Africa. 
The meeting discussed FSB’s main areas of 
strategic focus. These included FSB’s ongoing 
and planned work for 2025, as well as how RCG 
SSA members could contribute to the work 
programme. Specific topics explored included 
cross-border payments, regional economic 
outlook and prospects, as well as climaterelated risks. Members resolved to continue 
their collaborative monitoring efforts towards 
promoting regional and global f inancial 
stability.
5.2.7 IMF East Africa Technical Assistance 
Centre (East-AFRITAC)
IMF’s East Africa Technical Assistance Centre 
(East-AFRITAC) is a Technical Assistance (TA) 
arm of the International Monetary Fund (IMF). 
Its objective is to enhance financial sector 
capacity in seven Eastern Africa countries 
(Eritrea, Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Rwanda, 
Tanzania, Uganda, and South Sudan).
In 2025, CBK benefited from East AFRITAC’s 
Technical Assistance (TA) and capacity building 
in various areas aimed at developing robust 
banking sector regulatory and supervisory 
frameworks. Specifically, a follow-up Technical 
Assistance (TA) mission on Basel III Liquidity 
Standards and Leverage Ratio to the Central 
Bank of Kenya (CBK) in May 2025. The objective 
of the TA was to train CBK staff on the 
implementation and analysis of returns on 
draft guidelines on Liquidity Coverage Ratio 
(LCR), Net Stable Funding Ratio (NSFR) and 
Leverage Ratio (LR). The Basel III framework 
is a prudential framework developed by the 
Basel Committee for Banking Supervision for 
Banking Institutions, that provides guidance 
to banks on prudential standards.

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 87
REGIONAL AND INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENTS AND INITIATIVES
5.2.8 The African Rural and Agricultural 
Credit Association (AFRACA)
The African Rural and Agricultural Credit 
Association (AFRACA) is a pan-African network 
of financial and non-financial institutions 
dedicated to improving access to rural and 
agricultural finance. Headquartered in Nairobi, 
Kenya, with its Secretariat hosted at the CBK–
IMS Institute, AFRACA promotes financial 
inclusion for smallholder farmers and agriSMEs through policy innovation, knowledge 
sharing, capacity development, and strategic 
partnerships across Africa. In its quest to 
promote inclusive finance in Africa, AFRACA 
held the following events in 2025:
a) CBK participated in the 92nd ExCo Meeting 
of AFRACA, that was held in Bujumbura, 
Burundi, on May 5-6, 2025. The meeting 
reviewed progress in preparations for the 8th
World Congress on Rural and Agricultural 
Finance, scheduled for October 2025 in 
Mombasa, Kenya, and considered strategies 
to strengthen member participation and 
international engagement. Deliberations 
also focused on enhancing AFRACA’s 
governance framework, strengthening 
resource mobilization efforts, and ensuring 
the Association’s long-term sustainability, 
including providing strategic input into 
the development of AFRACA’s 2026–2030 
Strategic Plan.
b) The Central Bank of Kenya (CBK), in 
partnership with AFRACA, successfully 
co-hosted the 8th World Congress on Rural 
and Agricultural Finance in Mombasa 
f rom October 27–31, 2025. Held every 
three years, the World Congress is a global 
forum convened by continental rural and 
agricultural finance associations. Previous 
editions were hosted in New Delhi, India, 
and Morelia, Mexico.
The 2025 World Congress marked the 
first time the event was held in Africa, 
attracting more than 250 delegates from 
46 countries across Africa, Latin America, 
Asia, and Europe. Under the theme, 
“Building Resilient Local Agrifood Systems: 
The Role of Robust Financing Mechanisms 
and Policy,” the Congress explored 
transformative approaches to rural and 
agricultural finance, with a particular 
focus on digital innovation, inclusive 
financial policies, and risk management 
instruments such as crop insurance, credit 
guarantee schemes, and blended finance.
Alongside the World Congress, AFRACA 
convened its 93rd Executive Committee 
(ExCo) Meeting and 23rd General Assembly. 
Key deliberations included the presentation, 
deliberation, and adoption of AFRACA’s 
2026–2030 Strategic Plan by the ExCo. The 
General Assembly also conducted elections 
for ExCo members for the 2026–2027 term, 
during which the Bank of Uganda was 
elected Chairperson of AFRACA.
These events provided an opportunity 
for AFRACA members to forge strategic 
partnerships and synergies, which are 
important in shaping AFRACA’s future activities.
5.2.9 Bank Supervision Application (BSA)
a) Background
The Bank Supervision Application (BSA) is 
a web-based software, facilitating offsite 
surveillance and onsite examination of financial 
institutions, to ensure a safe and sound 
financial system. The system was developed 
through a joint initiative of Eastern, Central and 
Sothern Africa central banks to support the 
automation of Banking Supervision functions. 
Currently, the BSA application is being used 

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 88
REGIONAL AND INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENTS AND INITIATIVES
by 1828 (eighteen) central banks and two 
regulatory authorities.
The BSA system is managed by Bank 
Supervision Application Support Office (BSO) 
domiciled at the Central Bank of Mozambique. 
BSO is responsible for BSA development, 
maintenance and providing support to BSA 
users. 
BSO, as the lead developer of BSA together 
with the central banks using the system have 
continuously improved the BSA software. The 
software has evolved from BSA Version 1.0 in 
2003 to the current BSA Version 5.0 launched 
in 2024.
b) BSA Governance
The functioning of the BSA System is governed 
by the BSA Stakeholders and the BSA Executive 
Committee. The BSA Stakeholders are the 
Bank Supervision and Information Technology 
Directors from fourteen BSA founding Central 
Banks. The BSA Executive is composed of four 
central banks elected on a rotational basis from 
among the BSA Stakeholders every three years. 
The BSA Stakeholders and the BSA Executive 
Committee are responsible for strategic 
management of all BSA related activities.
c) BSA Activities in 2025
The BSO organized several ordinary meetings 
in 2025 which included the first BSA Executive 
Committee (BEC), hosted by Banco de 
Moçambique, held from April 10 -11, 2025, 
in Maputo, Mozambique. The second BEC 
meeting was held from September 17 -18, 2025 
28 Banque de la République du Burundi, Banco de Cabo Verde, 
, Banque Centrale du Congo, Central Bank of the Republic 
of Djibouti, National Bank of Ethiopia, Central Bank of Haiti, 
Central Bank of Kenya, Central Bank of Lesotho, Central Bank 
of the Republic of Madagascar, Reserve Bank of Malawi, 
Banco de Moçambique, Bank of South Sudan, Bank of 
Uganda, Bank of Zambia, Reserve Bank of Zimbabwe, Central 
Bank of the Democratic Republic of Timor-Leste, Central 
Bank of Eswatini, Financial Services Regulatory Authority of 
Eswatini, Central Bank of Haiti, Central Bank of Comoros, and 
Mozambican Insurance Supervision Institute.
and the annual BSA Stakeholders Meeting was 
held on September 19, 2025. Both meetings 
were hosted by the Reserve Bank of Zimbabwe 
and held in Victoria Falls, Zimbabwe. The 
main objective of the referred meetings was 
to review the BSO operational plan progress 
and the BSO strategic plan implementation. 
The BSA Stakeholders meeting also considered 
and approved the 2026 operational plan.
The BSA User Group meeting was held in 
Addis Ababa, Ethiopia, from July 7 - 11, 2025. 
The meeting was hosted by the National Bank 
of Ethiopia. The BSA Users Training was held 
from November 17 - 21, 2025, and was hosted 
by Banco de Moçambique and held in Maputo. 
The meeting and the training were held to 
review new enhancements, user challenges 
and solutions in the use of the BSA system.
5.2.10 Institute of International Finance (IIF)
CBK marked nine years since joining the 
International Institute of Finance (IIF) as an 
Associate Member. Over the previous eight 
years, the Bank has benefited from access to 
IIF’s wide research and publications focusing 
on a broad range of topical issues relevant to 
CBK’s regulatory mandate. In 2025, the Bank 
continued accessing content from the IIF’s 
portal on a wide range of emerging areas 
of supervisory significance, with a view to 
enhancing staff skills and competencies.
5.2.11 Financial Stability Institute (FSI)
In 2025, CBK maintained its subscription to 
the Financial Stability Institute’s (FSI) on-line 
learning tool, FSI Connect. Under FSI Connect, 
staff can pursue on-line courses in various areas 
of financial regulation. The courses apprise 
users of both the theoretical and practical 
aspects of financial regulation for continued 
learning and capacity development. 

CENTRAL BANK OF KENYA
BANK SUPERVISION ANNUAL REPORT 2025 89
REGIONAL AND INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENTS AND INITIATIVES
In 2025, 5 members of BSD’s technical staff 
participated in BIS-IMF Supervisory and 
Regulatory Online Course (SROC) for Banking 
Supervisors offered through the FSI Connect
Platform. The course is an interactive on-line 
programme organised jointly between the 
Bank of International Settlements (BIS) and 
the International Monetary Fund (IMF) from 
September 2025 through February 2026. The 
programme updated participants on the 
technical aspects of various supervisory topics. 
5.2.12 Islamic Financial Services Board (IFSB)
CBK joined the Islamic Financial Services 
Board (IFSB) in 2019 as an Associate Member. 
IFSB is an international standard-setting 
organization based in Kuala Lumpur, Malaysia 
for the Islamic financial services industry. 
It promotes the soundness and stability of 
the industry, by issuing global prudential 
standards and guidance for Islamic banking, 
Islamic capital markets and Takāful (Insurance) 
sectors. Over the previous four years, the Bank 
has participated in surveys and conferences 
that focus on Islamic banking which is relevant 
to CBK’s regulatory mandate. In the year 2025, 
CBK kept abreast of developments in IFSB’s 
Standards and Guidance as updated through 
IFSB’s virtual information dissemination 
channels. 
5.2.13 Network for Greening the Financial 
System (NGFS)
•	 In April 2022, CBK officially became a 
member of the Network for Greening the 
Financial System (NGFS), to benefit from 
the membership of NGFS by learning from 
and contributing to global efforts on Green 
Finance. NGFS is a voluntary, peer-to peer 
network of Central Banks and regulators 
whose purpose is to share best practices 
and build technical capacity to address 
climate-related and environmental risks 
to the financial system. The Network also 
seeks to mobilize capital to support the 
transition towards a sustainable economy. 
•	 NGFS delivers its objectives through 
workstreams, task forces and expert 
networks. The current workstreams 
comprise:-
a) The workstream on supervision, 
which promotes sound supervisory 
practices for addressing climaterelated and nature-related financial 
risks.
b) The workstream on Scenario Design 
and Analysis, which develops and 
enhances climate and nature-related 
scenario analysis methodologies.
c) The workstream on Monetary Policy,
which examines the implications of 
climate and environmental risks for 
monetary policy f rameworks and 
implementation.
d) The workstream on Central Bank 
Operations, which supports the 
integration of climate considerations 
into central bank operations and 
institutional practices. 
To this end, the Network defines and coordinates 
the implementation of best practices in 
climate-risk management across member 
jurisdictions. To enhance its participation 
in NGFS activities, CBK designated two 
representatives to contribute to the NGFS 
workstreams on Financial Supervision and 
Scenario Design and Analysis.

In an effort to enhance its relationship with

working relationships between regulators and

information.

5.3 Kenyan Banks Regional Footprint
Some Kenyan banks have expanded in the 
East African Community (EAC) Partner States. 
These banks include, KCB Group Holdings Plc, 
Diamond Trust Bank Group, NCBA Group Plc, 
Guaranty Trust Bank Kenya Limited, Equity 
Group Holdings Plc, I&M Group PLC, African 
Banking Corporation Limited and The Cooperative Bank of Kenya Limited.
They have positioned themselves to capitalize 
on the growing cross-border trade flows. This 
has in turn contributed to the deepening of 
customer relationships, the delivery of products 
and services. It has also led to utilization of 
host country resources, both capital and 
human, that has positively impacted market 
development and social economics.
The total number of branches of Kenyan banks 
subsidiaries in EAC Partner states and DRC 
increased by 1.0 percent from 586 branches 
recorded as at December 31, 2024, to 592 
branches recorded as at December 31, 2025. 
The increase was mainly driven by opening of 
new branches by KCB Group, in Tanzania and 
Uganda. The regional presence is as illustrated 
in Table 29. In addition to having presence 
within the EAC Partner States, some of the 
Kenyan banks such as I&M Group PLC and

the EAC jurisdictions as follows: -

5.3.1.1 Number of Employees
The subsidiaries of Kenyan banks had a combined employee count of 13,258 as at December 31, 
2025, an increase of 3.5 percent compared to 12,811 employees in the preceding year. The growth 
was primarily driven by Equity Group Holdings Plc operations in Uganda and DRC. The employee 
count in subsidiaries shows that DRC had the highest number of employees at 4,032 as at December 
2025 compared to 3,754 employees in 2024. DRC accounted for 30.4 percent of the total number 
of employees and has the largest proportion of subsidiaries and branches in the region.

5.3.1.3 Gross Loans 
The combined gross loans for the subsidiaries 
in the host countries stood at Ksh.1,132 billion as 
at December 31, 2025, an increase of 17 percent 
from Ksh.967.68 billion as at December 31, 2024. 
The increase was mainly due to the growth in 
loans for Equity Group Plc’s subsidiary in DRC, 
Banque Commerciale Du Congo - BCDC. 
The Kenyan banks subsidiaries operating in 
DRC recorded the highest level of gross loans 
at Ksh.443.9 billion accounting for 39.2 percent 
of gross loans in all the subsidiaries outside 
Kenya, followed by subsidiaries operating in 
Tanzania which accounted for 19 percent of the 
gross loans. Rwanda and Uganda accounted 
for 17.9 percent and 14.9 percent of the gross 
loans respectively. Mauritius recorded gross 
loans of Ksh.73.6 billion accounting for 6.5 
percent of gross loans. Chart 17 below shows

5.3.1.4 Deposits
The Kenyan banks subsidiaries accounted for a combined deposit base of Ksh.1,829 billion in 
December 2025 compared to Ksh.1,702 billion in December 2024. The main contributors of deposits 
were Equity Group Plc’s subsidiary in DRC, Banque Commerciale Du Congo (BCDC) and KCB Group 
Plc’s subsidiary in DRC, Trust Merchant Bank. BCDC had total deposits of Ksh.537.2 billion or 29.3 per 
cent of the total deposits of subsidiaries in the host countries. On the other hand, Trust Merchant 
Bank had total deposits of Ksh.244.4 billion or 13.4 per cent of the total deposits of subsidiaries in 
the host countries. The regional spread of deposits is as depicted in Table 32.

5.3.1.5 Profitability
The regional subsidiaries’ profit before tax 
stood at Ksh.87.62 billion as at December 31, 
2025, an increase of 19.4 percent from Ksh.73.36 
billion reported in December 31, 2024. The 
increase was mainly due to higher net interest 
and non-funded income.
DRC contributed to the highest earning 
capacity, recording Ksh.43.2 billion in profit, 
translating to 49.3 percent of the total profits. 
Subsidiaries operating in Rwanda, Tanzania and 
Uganda contributed 18.8 percent, 15.3 percent 
and 10.1 per cent of the total subsidiaries profits 
respectively. 
The profits for DRC and Rwanda were driven 
by Equity Group Plc’s subsidiary , Banque 
Commerciale Du Congo -BCDC, and Equity 
Bank Rwanda Plc respectively. 
In Tanzania, the profits were driven by KCB 
Group Plc’s subsidiary in KCB Bank Tanzania 
Plc. Equity Group Plc’s subsidiary in DRC, 
Banque Commerciale Du Congo -BCDC profits 
in DRC accounted for 36.5 percent of the overall 
subsidiaries profits.
Subsidiaries operating in South Sudan, 
Mauritius and Burundi were the least profitable 
and contributed 3.4 percent, 2 percent and 1.1 
percent of the total profits respectively. The 
profitability of the banks in their respective 
host countries is as shown in Chart 18.
Chart 18: Regional Profitability
